#7354. Senior Forward Deployed Engineer at Clutch

Clutch builds AI agents that work on behalf of credit unions. Emma, our collections agent, is live at five credit unions and handled 60,000+ member conversations in her first twelve weeks at one of them. Behind her is a growing portfolio: origination, fraud step-up, member (re)engagement and activation agents, all built on the same Agents Platform (TypeScript, NestJS, our own agent SDK, MCP, Temporal, promptfoo).

As a FDE you are one of the two engineers on an agent. You take it from "sold" to "live and performing" at a specific credit union: you sit in the discovery sessions, you build the integrations to their core banking and loan systems, you author and tune the agent's behavior, you run the live tests with their staff, and you stay with it through go-live and the first weeks of production. When something needs a person in the room, you are the one who flies.

This is a senior engineering role, not a solutions or support role. You write production code in our monorepo every week: integrations, modules, and the agents themselves (prompts, tools, evals), together with the other engineers on the pod. The difference from a traditional engineer is that you are on site more often, you make judgment calls in front of the customer, and you own the outcome. What you learn in the field flows straight back into the product: defaults, playbooks, and the platform capabilities the next agent will need.

You build; our AI Operations Managers deliver. The pod (pod lead plus engineers) designs and builds each agent in a high-touch way with the first customers. Once the agent is defined and live, AI Ops coordinates the implementations, configuration and ongoing success at the next credit unions. You hand them something that works and a playbook to run it.

This role requires roughly 20% of travel on average, with one- to two-week stretches around kickoffs and go-lives. Based in the US, able to travel on short notice

You'll join the Agents team, a small, senior group operating as a startup inside Clutch: seven engineers, a dedicated AI product group, and AI sales specialists, working directly with some of the largest credit unions in the country. Every agent is staffed by a pair of engineers, one leaning toward the conversation and LLM side, one toward software and integrations, with a pod lead who owns the product day to day. There is no layer between you and the customer, and no layer between you and the platform team.

The operating style is direct, written, and evidence-driven: BLUF updates, evals over opinions, business outcomes over ticket counts. The team is spread across Brazil, Argentina and the US and works async by default. Being on site is a tool we use at specific moments (kickoff, live testing, go-live), not a permanent posting.

Ship your first production change to a live agent through the team's review process, and be able to explain how a member's call flows through the Agents Platform end to end

Learn one agent deeply: system prompt, tool contracts, orchestration triggers, compliance gates, and the implementation process from kickoff to go-live

Join at least one credit union implementation as the second engineer, including a live testing session with their staff

Map where the current implementation process loses time and where a default or a tool would have removed a round trip

Own the technical side of one credit union implementation end to end: core and LOS integration, agent configuration, testing, go-live, and the first weeks of production

Run the eval and tuning loop for your agent (golden transcripts, rubric judges, regression suite) and be trusted to change its behavior without a second engineer checking your work

Have completed at least two on-site visits and turned what you heard there into product decisions with your pod lead and PM

Contribute at least one reusable capability back to the Agents Platform that the next implementation will not have to rebuild

Have taken one net-new agent from sold to live at its first credit union, with data you can show the partner

Be the engineer credit union staff ask for by name when something in their deployment needs to change

Have written the implementation playbook for your agent well enough that the AI Ops team runs the second and third credit union without you

Be shaping what the next agents look like, because you have seen where the current ones break in the field

7+ years of software engineering in a fast-paced environment, preferably in SaaS and/or Fintech, with senior TypeScript and Node.js backend experience in a large codebase (NestJS, CQRS, schema-validated boundaries, dependency injection). We do not count years of AI experience; the field is too young for that to mean anything. We look at what you have shipped and how you reason about it

Integration and API engineering: secure service-to-service endpoints, third-party APIs, webhooks, and the patience to work with legacy banking systems that were not designed to be integrated

Security and auth judgment: OAuth and machine-to-machine auth, tenant-scoped access, secrets handling, no PII in logs. Our agents touch sensitive member data and operate under consumer-contact regulation

Customer presence: you can sit across from a VP of Collections, hear a request, and say "here is what I recommend and why", including when the answer is no. You are comfortable making calls in the room and owning them afterwards

Code-quality judgment under deadline pressure: you take on debt deliberately, name it, and pay it down, rather than gold-plating or leaving silent debt behind

Genuine appetite for LLM agents: eval discipline for non-deterministic systems, reading a system prompt like code, understanding that some decisions must never reach the model. Curiosity plus shipped work is enough; research depth is not required

Nice to have, explicitly not required: voice and telephony (LiveKit, Twilio), Temporal or event-driven orchestration, Python, core banking or LOS integration experience, credit union or banking domain knowledge

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

Unforgettable Off-Sites: Twice a year, bond with colleagues in exciting destinations, fostering teamwork and fresh ideas.

Paid Time Off: Enjoy flexible PTO days yearly for relaxation and rejuvenation.

Stock Options: Joining us means having a stake in our success, so you'll receive stock options as part of your compensation package.

Home Office Setup: Create your ideal workspace with a dedicated budget for home office essentials.

Work Trip Budget: Grow personally and professionally with a budget for work-related trips and co-working.

Health Coverage: Prioritize your well-being with comprehensive health and dental insurance plans.

Clutch is a revolutionary vertical SaaS company, proudly backed by Andreessen Horowitz (A16z), aimed at revolutionizing the way Credit Unions engage and change the lives of their members. As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt. Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

#7353. Pinterest announces partnership with Microsoft Ads

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The integration will see Pinterest ad placements extended across Microsoft’s partner platforms.

Pinterest has announced a new integration with Microsoft Advertising, which will make Pinterest ad placements available to Microsoft platform users.

Microsoft Ads is primarily focused on placements within Microsoft’s own ecosystem, but it also incorporates a range of partner sites and platforms, enabling advertisers to expand their promotions. And now, Pinterest will be added to that list of partner platforms.

As explained by Pinterest: “Microsoft Advertising customers’ campaigns will have access to Pinterest’s visual search environment through a custom contextual bidding model. Eligible Microsoft Advertising campaigns can access Pinterest's open-auction inventory across home feed, search, and related Pins, expanding opportunities to engage consumers throughout their discovery journeys.”

Pinterest added that targeting for its Microsoft Ads integration will be built on Pinterest search queries, contextual signals, and first-party intent data.

“[T]he model identifies relevant ad placements across Pinterest’s visual search adjacent inventory,” Pinterest said. “The integration uses Pinterest Intelligence, Pinterest’s AI-powered understanding of people’s interests, combining hundreds of billions of connections with visual-understanding technology and behavioral signals to surface personalized interests and anticipate what users may want next.”

Pinterest has been working to expand its ad business in order to boost its revenue potential. Pinterest's ad intake has been growing, but its third quarter revenue intake fell short of Wall Street expectations, while it's also encountered regulatory restrictions in the EU market.

Last month, Pinterest announced a new partnership with Magnite that will make Pinterest’s ad inventory available via multiple ad exchanges, including Yahoo DSP and Amazon Ads DVA+, as well as Google Display and Video 360.

The new partnership with Microsoft will feed even more ads into Pinterest’s system.

Pinterest said its ad inventory will initially be available via Microsoft Ads in North America, Australia and New Zealand.

#7352. Is GEO Visibility Delivering Business Value, Or Are We Measuring The Wrong Outcome? via @sejournal, @billhunt

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: State Of Agentic AEO For 2027: What’s Scaling & What’s Stalling

GEO visibility can hide gaps that keep buyers from shortlisting your brand, while Decision Coverage helps identify the evidence needed to earn recommendations.

Recent research is raising questions about the commercial value of AI visibility. It also exposes a problem with what we have chosen to measure.

Rand Fishkin recently asked whether AI visibility is becoming a marketing boondoggle. In a Whiteboard video shared on LinkedIn, he discussed several studies questioning the commercial returns from visibility in standalone AI assistants, while distinguishing those platforms from Google’s AI-powered search experiences.

I understand the skepticism. We have seen an extraordinary amount of enthusiasm for GEO measurement, accompanied by new tools, new metrics, and a growing expectation that brands should monitor how often they appear in AI-generated answers. Much of that work is useful. I want to know whether a company appears when prospective customers search for its category, how its capabilities are presented, and which competitors are recommended.

For some time, I have been concerned that we are investing heavily in tracking mentions, citations, and share of voice without giving enough attention to the decisions customers are asking AI systems to help them make. We seem to have accepted that being mentioned is inherently valuable, even when we cannot demonstrate that the mention helped a customer evaluate the company or move toward a purchase.

I’m also concerned about this given the executive attention SEO, GEO, and AEO are receiving. Search professionals have spent years trying to secure the resources and cross-functional cooperation needed to make meaningful changes. Now, in some organizations, we have senior leadership listening, budgets for tools and content, and an unusual willingness to bring marketing, product, sales, and technical teams together.

We risk squandering that opportunity if the principal outcome we report is more AI visibility. Executives are investing because they believe AI is changing how customers discover and choose vendors. We need to show that our work is addressing that change, not simply producing another set of exposure metrics.

The starting point is the customer’s motivation. People generally aren’t going to ChatGPT, Perplexity, or Google’s AI-powered search experiences for another page of links. They want help understanding something, comparing alternatives, or reaching a decision. Traditional search left much of that work to the user, who had to visit multiple sites and reconcile the information. AI systems are attractive partly because they reduce that effort.

Yet we still talk about GEO as though the expected journey is a brand mention, followed by a click, followed by a conversion. That sequence may describe some purchases, but it is a poor model for many complex B2B decisions.

Fishkin’s argument drew on research from Marie Haynes, The SEO Works, and Kevin Indig. Although they approached the issue differently, their findings make it difficult to assume that increasing visibility in standalone AI assistants will necessarily produce meaningful traffic or conversions.

In GEO Is a Distraction, Haynes examined analytics data from 100 websites representing more than 145 million sessions. ChatGPT referrals accounted for approximately 0.62% of Google organic sessions, rising to 0.73% when other tracked language models were included. Even when ChatGPT visitors converted at higher rates, the difference in traffic volume meant that Google organic traffic remained far more important to measured conversions.

Her concern is partly one of priorities. Given the changes taking place within Google Search, how much effort should businesses devote to improving visibility in standalone assistants that currently contribute relatively little measurable traffic?

The SEO Works’ AI Referral Traffic Benchmark examined 95 websites and found that organic search generated 39 times as many sessions as AI assistants. For every 100 conversions attributed to organic search, AI referrals generated 3.2. The study also found considerable variation in conversion performance. Although AI referrals converted approximately 18% better overall, the rate for B2B product companies was only 0.62 times that of organic traffic.

That last finding caught my attention because B2B buying behavior is often discussed as though AI-assisted discovery will naturally produce exceptionally valuable visits. I agree it may, but only if the information is available, and I note that it might not be universally applicable.

Kevin Indig explored another possibility in Does AI Drive More Direct Traffic?. Perhaps AI influences buyers who subsequently visit a website directly or search for a brand, leaving the original influence unattributed. In an analysis of 12 analytics properties, he found that some apparent direct-traffic growth was due to bot activity. Additional testing across four brands and 632 tests did not establish a reliable relationship between AI visibility and subsequent direct or branded search activity.

Indig did not argue that AI has no influence on purchasing behavior. Rather, his work demonstrated how difficult it is to establish that influence using the signals available in conventional analytics.

I think these findings deserve more than a debate about whether GEO is worth the investment. They should prompt us to reconsider what we are asking visibility metrics to prove.

If a customer uses AI to compare vendors, rule out unsuitable products, or validate a shortlist, there may be no referral click. The customer could make substantial progress toward a decision without visiting any of the cited sources. That does not mean the AI interaction had no value, but it also does not mean every brand mentioned in the response benefited commercially.

The question I want to answer is what happened during the evaluation, particularly whether the company had enough supporting evidence to be considered a suitable choice.

See also: AI Visibility Measurement: What To Track & What To Ignore

A recent conversation with an executive brought this into focus for me. He was evaluating outside consulting support and explained that his organization had been disappointed by consultants and vendors three times in the previous year. He was not interested in another polished sales pitch or a broad claim of expertise. Having been burned repeatedly, he intended to make the next selection process considerably more demanding.

What stood out was his sense of responsibility for the decision. He needed to be confident that the next consultant could deliver, but he also needed to justify that confidence to the organization.

We sometimes underestimate how much of a complex purchasing decision is about avoiding risk. Buyers are trying to establish that a product will work in their environment, meet the requirements of different stakeholders, and avoid creating problems they will later have to explain. When budgets are tight, the consequences of getting that decision wrong become even more significant.

A company can be well-known, highly visible, and frequently mentioned without addressing those concerns.

Consider a buyer evaluating specialized software. Knowing that a vendor is a recognized provider may be useful at the beginning of the process. Once the buyer introduces requirements involving integrations, compliance, implementation capacity, or operational constraints, general category visibility becomes much less informative. The buyer needs evidence that the vendor can satisfy those requirements.

This is where I believe the distinction between appearing in an answer and earning a recommendation becomes commercially important.

See also: 3 Steps To Win B2B Brand Consideration, Day Zero Decides The Shortlist

We saw this problem in an engagement with a specialized B2B software company. Across the AI responses we observed, the brand was cited 98% of the time. By most visibility measures, that would be considered an excellent result. The company was clearly being recognized within its category.

At the same time, it was receiving little observable AI referral traffic, and its lead volume was declining. We could not establish that AI had caused the decline, but the situation prompted us to look more closely at what happened when buyers moved beyond broad-category questions.

We began testing more specific decision scenarios, introducing the requirements that a prospective customer might use to determine which vendors to include on a shortlist. Under those conditions, the company’s recommendation rate was 17%. In a separate testing condition where supporting evidence was absent, the rate was 8%.

As we examined the responses, we found that important capabilities were not always supported by information the AI systems could retrieve. In a workshop reviewing those gaps, someone on the client team said, “We have all of that. We just don’t have it on the website.”

I have heard variations of that statement throughout my career, but it takes on a different significance when customers can use AI to evaluate vendors before speaking with sales.

There are often legitimate reasons information is not publicly available. Some details are commercially sensitive. Some capabilities are complex enough that sales or technical teams prefer to explain them directly. Other information simply never makes it onto the website because nobody has recognized its importance to the buying decision.

Historically, a website could generate interest and leave the lead nurturing to sales. As research has shown, especially in B2B, AI-assisted evaluation often takes place before the buyer makes contact, creating a significant disadvantage. The company may satisfy the requirement, yet the available evidence may not be sufficient to establish that suitability.

The answer is not to just publish everything but to clearly identify the requirements customers are likely to evaluate and determine what credible, appropriately disclosed evidence can support those assessments.

See also: B2B Buyers Choose A Vendor Before They Reach Out – 3 Ways To Be Visible When It Counts

This is the thinking behind the Decision Coverage framework I introduced in Decision Coverage: Why AI Recommends Some Brands And Not Others.

In an earlier article, When AI Takes The Click, Click Worthiness Should Guide Your Strategy, I argued that marketers should focus more attention on interactions where direct engagement still adds meaningful value after AI has answered an informational question. Decision Coverage approaches the problem from the other side: before a customer engages directly, have we made enough evidence available for our company to be evaluated accurately?

One useful concept is the eligibility gate, a requirement that a solution must meet to be considered suitable for a particular buyer. This is a way to model purchasing decisions, not a claim that language models use a fixed internal checklist.

A buyer might regard some capabilities as desirable and others as mandatory. If a vendor cannot satisfy a critical requirement, it may not belong on the shortlist, regardless of how visible it is. But there is another possibility that marketers need to recognize: the vendor meets the requirement, yet the supporting evidence is missing or difficult to retrieve.

Traditional content-gap analysis does not always uncover this problem. We may find that a competitor has written about a topic we have not covered, or that our pages need stronger explanations of a product category. Those findings can improve discoverability, but they may have little bearing on whether a buyer can establish that we meet a mandatory requirement.

Decision Coverage starts with the decision itself. What is the customer trying to accomplish? Who is involved? Which requirements are essential, which are negotiable, and what trade-offs are likely to influence the choice? We can then examine whether the information available about the company is sufficient to support an assessment against those criteria.

This requires significantly more cooperation across the business than many traditional SEO activities. Marketing may understand the buyer persona, but product teams often hold the evidence needed to answer them, and most importantly, sales understands the prospects’ objections and evaluation criteria.

That is another reason I am concerned about wasting the current level of executive attention. We finally have an opportunity to bring these groups together around a problem that matters to customers. I would rather use that access to close important evidence gaps than simply produce more category content.

Our research into AI fan-out questions gave us another reason to focus on decision criteria. In one study beginning with a prompt asking for the best solution in a software category, 62% of the observed questions concerned understanding the category, developing evaluation criteria, or comparing vendors.

Those observations do not reveal a model’s complete internal reasoning, and I would not suggest that every AI system follows the same process. What they show is that the observable research supporting a recommendation can involve considerably more than retrieving a list of vendors.

We also found that recommendations sometimes changed when we altered the buyer’s perspective. A prompt framed around an individual’s professional responsibilities did not always produce the same recommendations as one written from the perspective of a department or organization.

In another test, a vendor appeared as the leading recommendation until we changed the emphasis and ordering of the requirements. That vendor then disappeared from the recommended set, replaced by a company that had not previously appeared but was particularly strong on the newly emphasized criterion.

I cannot attribute that change solely to the order of the requirements, as we saw other cases where retests days later prompted different interpretations, putting focus on different criteria. Nevertheless, the results made it difficult to defend the idea that recommendation performance could be represented adequately by a single broad prompt or a static collection of category queries.

That is why understanding decision context matters. A company may be the strongest option for one combination of requirements and less suitable when another constraint takes priority. Unfortunately, we don’t have unlimited budgets for every conceivable prompt, but we can maybe use larger diagnostic prompts that capture multiple prompt turns.

Accordingly, for measurement and alignment purposes, I would rather test a representative set of buyer decisions than simply increase the number of prompts being tracked. The objective is to understand when the company qualifies, where evidence is missing, and which competing vendors are better supported for particular requirements. Those are the critical insights we need to understand to truly close gaps.

Mike King also raises a related measurement issue in How Do We Move Forward if the SERP Is Generative?. As search results become dynamically generated, he argues that conventional assumptions about stable rankings are increasingly difficult to maintain. Measuring visibility requires repeated observations and a probabilistic approach rather than relying on a single result.

I agree, and I think recommendation measurement needs to account for another source of variation: the customer’s requirements.

A company might appear in nearly every broad category response but be recommended infrequently for the decision scenarios that matter most to its business. Another company might have lower overall visibility but be consistently recommended when its particular strengths align with the buyer’s needs. Aggregated mention counts cannot adequately explain either situation.

For our B2B software client, the evidence gaps suggested a testable hypothesis. If the company genuinely satisfies important buyer requirements but has not made that information retrievable, improving the available evidence should increase its recommendation frequency under comparable decision scenarios.

We can establish a baseline, address specific gaps, and repeat the testing. Because generated responses vary, that work needs multiple observations and consistent scenarios rather than a few favorable examples.

I want to be careful about what an improvement would mean, however. A higher recommendation rate would suggest that the available evidence is better supporting the company’s suitability. It would not prove that buyers are choosing the company more often or that the work has increased revenue.

That distinction matters especially for this client, whose sales cycle typically lasts 9 to 14 months. Even if better evidence starts influencing evaluations now, we may not see the corresponding effect on qualified opportunities or pipeline for several quarters. Some buyers may already have made their decisions while important information was missing.

We also have to recognize that the decline in leads could have several causes. Market demand, product competitiveness, sales execution, and other marketing activity may all be influencing performance. The combination of declining leads and weak recommendation performance is a reason to investigate, not evidence of causation.

I would therefore measure progress in stages. First, determine whether the gaps in decision-support evidence have actually been closed. Then test whether recommendation performance changes in the scenarios where those gaps mattered. Over time, examine whether qualified opportunities, pipeline, and eventually revenue show corresponding improvements, while considering other factors that could explain the results.

Customer interviews and self-reported discovery information may help fill some of the attribution gaps. They will not provide perfect answers, but they can help us understand whether AI-assisted research played a role in the buying process. This is consistent with Indig’s broader finding that neither referral traffic nor changes in direct traffic can explain AI’s commercial influence on their own.

I would not make AI referral traffic the primary KPI for this work. If the customer can reach an evaluation or shortlist decision without clicking, referral counts may tell us relatively little about whether our information helped or hindered that decision.

I think Fishkin is right to challenge the industry on business value. The findings from Haynes, The SEO Works, and Indig should make marketers cautious about presenting increased AI visibility as evidence of commercial success. King’s work also reminds us that the visibility we are trying to measure is becoming less stable and more dependent on context.

My concern is that we will respond by building increasingly sophisticated visibility dashboards without addressing what happens when a buyer asks whether a company is actually suitable.

For complex purchases, that question is often more important than whether the brand appeared in an earlier answer. Customers are trying to reduce risk, compare alternatives, and make defensible decisions. If AI systems are helping them do that work, our responsibility is to make sure the information available about our companies supports an accurate evaluation.

This is also an opportunity for search professionals to expand their contribution. The attention GEO is receiving has created access to people and resources that were often difficult to engage through conventional SEO initiatives. We can use that access to work with product, sales, technical, and legal teams to identify what customers need to establish and make appropriate evidence available.

I do need to know whether a brand is being discovered and represented accurately, but more importantly, I want to know whether the brand is recommended when it genuinely meets a buyer’s requirements, what evidence supports that recommendation, and where missing information may be limiting consideration.

If we can improve those leading indicators and then establish, over time, that they contribute to higher-quality opportunities and better commercial outcomes, we will have a much stronger argument for GEO investment than mention counts alone can provide.

The question is not whether AI visibility has value. It is whether we are confusing being present in an answer with being considered in a decision.

#7350. Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit

LiveKit is building the infrastructure layer for the voice-driven era of computing. Our platform gives developers everything they need to build, test, deploy, scale, and observe agents in production. Founded in 2021, LiveKit powers voice AI applications for OpenAI, xAI, Salesforce, Coursera, Spotify, and thousands of others, collectively facilitating billions of calls each year.

The Staff GTM Systems Engineer at LiveKit is the technical owner and architect of the systems that power our go-to-market engine. You'll decide how our GTM stack is designed, what we build versus buy, and where we invest next — and you'll be hands-on building it: Salesforce architecture, integrations, enrichment pipelines, and the agentic workflows that help Sales and Marketing move faster and smarter.

Salesforce is our system of record for revenue, pipeline, and customer data. HubSpot is our marketing layer for campaigns and lead generation, and what it captures needs to flow cleanly and reliably into Salesforce. Around them sits a growing stack of enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and data platform tools. You'll own the architecture that ties it all together: disciplined change management, observable pipelines, data models built for AI consumption, and agentic workflows that act on GTM signals at scale — designed to serve the business for years, not quarters.

This role sits at the intersection of GTM, Data, Finance, and Engineering. You'll work directly with senior leaders to shape the GTM systems roadmap, turn business strategy into technical plans, and guide decisions that span multiple teams. You'll also help grow the engineers around you, setting the technical standards and practices the team builds on.

You understand the difference between owning a system and gatekeeping one. Sales lives in Salesforce. Marketing lives in HubSpot. Your job is to make sure both are governed well enough that others can work in them effectively, and powerful enough that the GTM team can do things they couldn't do before.

You'll thrive as a Staff GTM Systems Engineer if you:

Are known as the go-to person for tough technical problems, and have good judgment about which ones matter most.

Think in systems and long time horizons, and can make confident architectural decisions with incomplete information.

Can clearly explain complex technical concepts and tradeoffs to engineers and executives alike.

Believe systems should be managed with engineering discipline.

Ship fast and reliably — your work holds up in production.

Are a fast learner, frequently picking up new languages and tools.

Find real satisfaction in mentoring other engineers and helping them grow.

Own the architecture of the GTM systems stack: define how Salesforce, HubSpot, enrichment, sales intelligence, billing, and the data platform fit together, and write the technical strategy that guides how it evolves.

Lead build-vs-buy and procurement decisions: evaluate vendors, run proofs of concept, assess security and integration fit, and partner with Director of GTM Systems and Analytics, Finance, Legal, and IT on selection and renewals.

Shape the GTM systems roadmap with senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, RevOps, Finance, and Engineering: align on requirements, sequence investments, make clear tradeoffs, and lead cross-functional initiatives to completion.

Own Salesforce as the engineering system of record for revenue: data model, flows and automation, validation rules, field governance, and access control.

Own HubSpot for campaigns and lead generation, along with the HubSpot-Salesforce sync — so leads, contacts, and engagement signals reach the revenue pipeline without drift or silent failures.

Build and maintain integrations across the GTM stack, and partner with GTM Analytics Engineering on reliable data flows to the central data platform.

Design and deploy agentic workflows that consume GTM data, surface actionable signals, and automate manual operations — and set the patterns and guardrails others build on.

Establish observability and change management for critical GTM systems: alerts, dashboards, and runbooks; reviewed code changes, documented config, and rollback paths.

Mentor engineers across experience levels and set the technical standards for the team.

Partner with the Billing Systems Engineer to automate the CPQ-to-Billing handoff.

Extensive engineering experience in GTM or business systems, including owning a GTM stack's architecture end to end — build-vs-buy calls, vendor evaluations, and systems designed for a multi-year horizon.

Deep, hands-on Salesforce expertise — you understand the data model, have built complex flows and automation, and you think about it as an engineering system, not an admin tool. This is the primary bar.

HubSpot experience in a marketing context, including the HubSpot-Salesforce sync and what breaks and why.

Technical depth beyond the UI — you've built integrations with APIs, webhooks, and event streams, and you know when a native tool (a Flow, a Clay table, a HubSpot workflow) is the right answer versus when it's time to write code.

Agentic workflow experience — you've designed and deployed AI agents that act on GTM data in production.

Strong change management practices — you've shipped changes to live systems while protecting active deals and campaigns.

Experience managing a GTM tooling budget or vendor portfolio.

An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

#7349. Pinterest simplifies parental controls

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The platform has updated its parental control dashboard to offer a more streamlined way to manage devices and access options.

Pinterest has updated its parental control elements to offer a more integrated, streamlined process and make it easier for parents to manage their child’s in-app experience.

As shown in these example screens, Pinterest’s updated parental controls dashboard now includes all of the app’s oversight and management options in one place, including profile visibility, content recommendations and connection controls.

The updated parental controls also include direct links to manage device-level screen time and location settings. Parents will also be able to make their kids’ profiles private by default and set limits on ad personalization and social features.

In addition, Pinterest added tools that allow parents to control the amount of artificial intelligence-generated content that teens see in the app. There’s also new notification management functionality.

Parents can also decide whether their teens have autoplay video switched on or not, which could help to combat compulsive viewing behaviors.

Pinterest CEO Bill Ready said the platform is focused on building an experience that’s age-appropriate for teens from the start, with protections that are built in, “not bolted on.”

“For parents who want more involvement, the controls are easy to find and simple to use,” Ready said. “Families can choose the experience that is right for them, while teens can continue to explore their interests and feel inspired.”

Pinterest has been working to present its app as an alternative to potentially harmful, engagement-focused approaches that have been adopted by other social media platforms.

In September, Pinterest published a report on how teens use the app, which showed that more than 77% of its teen audience uses Pinterest to explore creative interests.

More teen control options will provide additional peace of mind for parents, which could help to further establish Pinterest as a safer space for young users.

#7347. ChatGPT Ads Are Growing Fast – But Are Advertisers Seeing Results? via @sejournal, @brookeosmundson

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: State Of Agentic AEO For 2027: What’s Scaling & What’s Stalling

Use qualified leads and sales to decide whether to keep spending on ChatGPT Ads, since cheap clicks and Google Search benchmarks can mislead.

OpenAI says more than 100,000 advertisers have run ads on ChatGPT. They’ve also reported that its ad business reached a $1 billion annualized revenue run rate less than 200 days after launch.

A recent Business Insider report found that some advertisers were struggling to earn back what they spent. Others reported results that matched or exceeded Google Ads.

In September, advertisers were still trying to figure out what a reasonable click cost looked like on ChatGPT Ads. Now that some have had time to see what those clicks turned into, they’re deciding whether the results justify keeping the campaigns running.

Business Insider cited Adthena’s findings from 30 enterprise brands that shared results between July and September. Adthena reported that 52% struggled to recover their spending, 26% matched or beat Google Ads, and 22% saw mixed results.

Adthena gives little detail on how each brand measured returns or which Google campaigns it used for comparison. A brand comparing lead costs could come away with a very different answer from one comparing closed sales. With 30 brands sharing results, there’s only so much those percentages can tell you about what to expect in your own account.

A LinkedIn poll I ran asked how people were using ChatGPT Ads. Of the 21 respondents, eight were still spending, and seven had tested and stopped. The other six wanted to test but couldn’t.

In the comments, advertisers shared what they ran into once their campaigns were live.

Melissa Mackey described the inquiries from her test as “junk leads.” Trevor Stolber tested the platform to prepare for client work and came away “very underwhelmed.” Leah Garcia said creative drift was her main challenge at a moderate spend level.

Brandi Benton ran into tracking issues during a short test and described targeting as “super limited.” Compared with Google Ads, she saw similar acquisition quality at a higher cost and with low volume. Her organic ChatGPT traffic brought in better-quality acquisitions than the ads. She plans to relaunch with new creative and give the next test more time.

I also had the opportunity to interview James Hamilton, co-founder of oakpool, a growth marketing agency and managed AI platform, about their ChatGPT Ads testing.

In our discussion, he told me most clients who joined the agency’s ChatGPT Ads pilot are still spending. Oakpool has been running campaigns since spring for clients in boutique hospitality, B2B services, and entertainment. Hamilton said ROAS has been lower than on Google, but enough to keep a small budget running.

OpenAI’s conversion pixel has worked well for the hospitality campaigns, Hamilton said, though he’d like more detail over time. He didn’t share spend or returns by industry. The results he described explain why clients have kept the campaigns running at their current budgets. How much more business those campaigns could bring in with additional spend is still open.

Hamilton still gets more questions from clients about appearing in organic ChatGPT answers than about buying ads:

Our clients ask us far more often about earning a place in ChatGPT’s answers than about buying one. We expect that balance to shift as the ad product matures.

Another agency advertiser said good organic results helped persuade some of its clients to try paid ads. Once the campaigns were live, some of those clients found that the ads brought in inquiries for work they couldn’t take.

An agency advertiser who asked to remain anonymous spent about $15,000 on its own ChatGPT Ads in the first month. The campaigns worked well at first, then results dropped off. The agency now spends about $800 a month and treats that budget more like branding.

About half of the agency’s clients who tried ChatGPT Ads stopped and moved their budgets back to Google. The advertiser cited conversion tracking issues and said it was hard to see where the ads appeared or why they showed up there.

One client, a bankruptcy lawyer, saw up to 20 conversions a month at a low cost per lead. After two months, the campaign hadn’t brought in a single paying client. The inquiries were related to the firm’s work, but they fell outside the kinds of cases the lawyer wanted to take.

OpenAI’s pixel and Conversions API can help advertisers connect form submissions to their ads. For a firm in this position, the intake notes are where you’d look next. If several callers expected a service the firm doesn’t offer, was there something in the ad or landing page that gave them that impression?

That’s something the team can check before spending more. If the offer was clear and the same inquiries kept coming in, it would help to know more about the conversations where the ads appeared.

See also: Should Your PPC Strategy Focus On The Lead Pipeline Or Revenue?

Gil Gildner said approvals took weeks and sometimes months. His team broadened the campaigns to get them running. Clicks averaged less than $1 in the first week, but early delivery appeared to skew toward lower-income markets the client hadn’t planned to prioritize.

The client had approved a sizable test budget, and Gildner doubted the team could spend it as planned. The first week gave him an average CPC for the traffic the campaign could buy. He still didn’t have a good sense of what clicks would cost in the markets the client wanted to test.

A working paper by Maximilian Kaiser and Christian Schulze examined 102 advertiser sites with full sales tracking. In the U.S., visits from ChatGPT Ads generated $0.89 in revenue per session, compared with $2.45 for Google Search ads. The revenue figures alone leave out what advertisers paid for those visits.

Say your Google Search campaign gets most of its conversions from people looking up your brand or checking prices. Using that CPA target for ChatGPT Ads assumes you’ll reach people who are just as ready to act. If you’re testing whether ChatGPT Ads can get your product onto a shortlist, a buyer might click weeks before they’re ready to book a demo. A CPA check at the end of the first week comes too early to account for those buyers.

For a B2B team, this is where it helps to tag the ad links separately from organic ChatGPT referrals. If tracking connects the visits, the team can see that a prospect first came through an ad and later returned through Google to fill out a form.

Keeping that earlier source in the CRM gives the team a way to follow the prospect past the demo request. It can check whether any of those leads became opportunities and how long that took.

To find out whether the ads generated opportunities the business would have missed otherwise, the team would need to compare outcomes with and without the ads running.

See also: Why Incrementality Testing Alone Won’t Fix Your Paid Media Budget – The Missing Metric

OpenAI’s technical breakdown explains how its ad system uses earlier messages to understand what someone is trying to do. The system uses that context to find relevant ads and weighs relevance alongside bids. As Search Engine Journal’s coverage of the auction explained, even an ad that wins the auction may never appear.

A B2B buyer might use ChatGPT to work out what features their team needs, then use Google to check vendor pricing. Matt Southern reported in September that people use both platforms. The audience overlap makes that path plausible. The research can’t tell advertisers whether an ad helped put a vendor on the shortlist.

OpenAI says advertisers don’t see users’ conversations. Even a broad breakdown of the types of conversations where ads ran would help, if OpenAI could provide it while keeping chats private.

A team seeing few demo requests could make a better call with that information. If people were still deciding what features they needed, the advertiser might try a landing page that helps them compare options. If the ads mostly appeared when people were already evaluating vendors, the team could check whether the page answered the questions buyers would have at that point, like what the product integrates with or how pricing works.

If you ran ads on Facebook in the early days, you’ve seen how much an ad platform can change. ChatGPT Ads is still in its first year. Comparing a first test with an account you’ve spent years improving can make it easy to write off a campaign before you know what it can do.

Some advertisers are already getting enough business from ChatGPT Ads to keep spending. There’s still little evidence that those results will hold across more advertisers or at higher budgets. And when a business knows why it keeps turning away the inquiries it paid for, giving the campaign another month is hard to justify without changing something.

As ChatGPT Ads matures, better delivery reporting and conversion tracking could give advertisers a reason to revisit some of the tests they’ve paused. A team that couldn’t tell why its ads kept drawing the wrong inquiries might be able to go back with a more specific test.

Until then, the results an advertiser can follow through to a sale or a qualified opportunity are a better basis for the next budget than the CPA from an account it’s been running for years.

#7345. TikTok highlights 2026 holiday trends

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Popular hashtags including #diychristmas and #pinkchristmas speak to a shift away from last year’s “Ralph Lauren Christmas” toward a more whimsical season.

TikTok has shared an overview of key holiday trends based on in-app activity, which could help marketers align with the popular shifts of the season. Which could be especially helpful given the role that TikTok now plays in defining popular culture.

Indeed, TikTok is now a critical influencer of music and movie trends, which also makes it a valuable guide on broader cultural shifts.

According to TikTok, “Whimsy Christmas” is a key trend this year. The trend offers a colorful, DIY-based take on the season.

As per TikTok: “Posts with #diychristmas are up over 80%, and TikTok users in the US are almost twice as likely as non-users to make their own decorations this season.”

TikTok also said that “Cozy Christmas” is another key trend centered around holiday movie nights, quiet winter evenings and cozy bedding. Posts with #cozychristmas are up over 170%.

Pink Christmas, which the app also calls “Pinksmas” is another trend of note.

“Posts with #pinkchristmas are up over 210%, illustrating the creativity behind the TikTok community driving interest in a ‘girly’ Christmas.”

Underlining these broader trends, TikTok added:

Some of these seasonal trends are to be expected, but the growth data could highlight opportunities for tie-ins to boost brand promotions.

TikTok also said that shopping-specific trends like #stockingstufferideas and #beautyadventcalendar are up significantly this month.

#7344. Senior Manager, Business Operations at Clutch

Clutch is at a pivotal moment. We're growing significantly year over year, and we need more connective tissue to keep pace. We're hiring a Senior Manager, Business Operations to connect the commercial side of the house, so the teams that win, onboard, and retain our customers work from the same facts and toward the same goals. This role sits in Finance.

This is a people manager role. You'll lead our Revenue Operations (RevOps) team and act as an operating partner to senior leaders across Sales, Marketing, Customer Success, Implementation, People, and Finance. Together with the RevOps team, you own the shared language of the business (definitions, data, and processes) so commercial teams make decisions from one view of it.

When something important doesn't have an owner yet, it lands with you: a partnership strategy, a new commercial model, or pricing and margin guidance for leadership. This is a fast-moving startup. We're building toward a much larger company but we aren't one yet, and most of this role has no playbook. You'll define the process, build the tool, and hand it to the team. If you want established structure, this likely isn't the role.

You'll report to the Director of Business Operations and work directly with senior leaders across the company as a neutral partner to every function rather than an advocate for one. Your work will be visible, and it will significantly influence how we make commercial decisions.

You'll lead a small, high-ownership RevOps team that runs the systems and data our commercial teams depend on, and creates leverage for them to do their jobs even better. The team doesn't just own systems. They build automation, tooling, and processes, and they're close partners to Sales and Marketing. You'll coach them, set priorities, and open up more strategic scope, while giving them real autonomy.

Learn the business end to end: the revenue funnel, the customer lifecycle, how each commercial team works day to day, and where decisions get stuck

Build trust with your RevOps team: learn the work they own, and set priorities and team rhythms together

Deliver the first version of client-level margin and cost-to-serve, the analysis that grounds our pricing strategy

Develop a first point of view on pricing and packaging for revenue leadership, built on that analysis

Put AI to work in your own workflow from week one, and identify the first set of manual, repetitive processes across the commercial teams worth automating

Stand up a first version of a deal desk, including an approval workflow, pricing and discount guardrails, and standard commercial terms

Partner with Sales and Marketing leaders as they prepare for the monthly business review: pressure-test their plans and point out risks, then solve them with the RevOps team by creating new processes, tooling, or automation

Build on the strength of the RevOps team: prioritize its roadmap against business outcomes and take on more strategic work together

Launch your first AI-enabled workflows or automations that remove manual reporting or reconciliation for the commercial teams, with time saved tracked

Have a team whose output and standards hold, with the processes, tooling, and automation you've built running without you in the room

Have AI-enabled, self-serve tooling that commercial teams use day to day, with measurable time savings

Be the person leadership trusts to pick up the next important thing that doesn't have an owner yet

5-8 years of total experience that combines consulting or banking with time as an operator inside a company

Experience managing people: you've coached ICs, given direct feedback, made tough talent calls, and built team rhythms

Startup experience. You've worked in a fast-moving, ever-changing environment where structure wasn't already there, and you built it. This is not a role for someone whose experience is only at large, established enterprises.

Strong analytical and financial modeling skills, including forecasting. You're very comfortable building models in spreadsheets from scratch, and they hold up to scrutiny. You use AI to move faster and sharpen your work, and you have the foundational modeling knowledge to know when its output is wrong.

Data-first and self-sufficient. You open the dataset before you build the slide, you're always looking for more data to test an idea, and you're comfortable getting into raw data yourself. You use AI to analyze it faster and know enough to check its work.

Commercial acumen: you can read commercial terms, spot the problematic ones, and reason about pricing, discounting, margin, and deal structure

A clear communicator who can walk an executive through a messy problem and leave them with a decision, and who is just as effective working alongside an AE, a CSM, or an engineer

Comfort with ambiguity. When something isn't set up yet, your instinct is to build it, not to wait for it. You create structure, manage competing priorities, and pivot when things change.

Relentless follow-through: you close loops, and people stop checking on what you've said you'll do

A partner's temperament: you challenge constructively without taking sides, you surface problems early, and you resolve cross-team conflict without escalating it

Industry experience in fintech, financial services, lending, banking, or credit unions

Please note that this role may evolve as our business needs change, so we appreciate your flexibility and adaptability.

Remote Flexibility: Enjoy the freedom of remote work from anywhere, balancing life and career seamlessly.

Unforgettable Off-Sites: Twice a year, bond with colleagues in exciting destinations, fostering teamwork and fresh ideas.

Paid Time Off: Enjoy flexible PTO days yearly for relaxation and rejuvenation.

As a champion of financial well-being, we address the urgent need for affordable lending solutions in an era where the average American grapples with over $155,000 in household debt.  Unlike traditional financial institutions, Clutch develops software to turn Credit Unions into FinTech lenders and leverage their balance sheets to responsibly lend to over 130M Americans. Our mission extends beyond mere financial transactions; we strive to fundamentally enhance the way credit unions interact with their members. By integrating cutting-edge technologies and user-centric designs, we help credit unions provide seamless digital experiences that are on par with leading tech companies. This approach not only preserves but revitalizes the longstanding tradition of community and member-focused service inherent to credit unions.

#7336. Meta launches iPad version of Edits

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The larger screen layout is intended to make it easier for users to manage video projects.

Meta has launched an iPad version of its Edits video editing app, while it's also added new tools and effects, as it continues to expand its video creation options.

The iPad launch will enable tablet users to edit their video clips on a larger screen.

As shown in this example screen, Edits for iPad has a bigger composition screen, which will make it easier to hone in on specific elements and access more editing options at one time.

It could be a valuable update for video creators. A MacOS version of Edits, which is the first desktop version of the app, launched earlier this month.

Edits also added a trim-upon-import option, which should make it easier to place clips into a project at the exact right time.

While it also added new filters and 1,600 new stickers. It's also experimenting with LUT file import options.

LUT files enable color conversion within video files, and this addition will provide more options in maintaining video style and format in the app.

Meta also expanded Edits’ artificial intelligence assistant capacity, with users now able to upload images and video into the chat and get recommendations on how to create a video from these sources.

Finally, there’s a new copyright check option in Edits on iOS, which will examine video projects for potential copyright concerns before they’re uploaded.

As shown in this image posted on X by app researcher Radu Oncescu, users will have five copyright checks available per day, which should help to avoid any restrictions or penalties.

These new tools expand the functionality of Edits and should provide more ways to build creative projects in the app.

#7333. AI Shopping Agents Bought A Fake Deal 37% Of The Time – Claude Opus Never Did via @sejournal, @slobodanmanic

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Where search leaders are putting 2027 budget, plus the AI visibility scorecard that replaces session reporting.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Nine AI models went Christmas shopping 6,156 times. They bought an obviously fake deal 37% of the time. Only Claude Opus never did.

AI shoppers trust the numbers a listing gives them. Even when they wrote that a deal looked suspicious, they often bought it anyway, and never asked me about it. I sent nine AI models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google, the companies behind Claude, ChatGPT, and Gemini, Christmas shopping 6,156 times. When one of the listings was an obviously fake deal, they bought it 37% of the time. Every purchase, with the reason the AI gave, is in the full study.

Every present had a shelf of 10 listings, and one of them was the fake: a perfect 5.0 from tens of thousands of reviews, at a fifth to a quarter of everyone else’s price. For the scented candle, that was $6 and a 5.0 from 39,000 reviews, next to real candles at $24 to $40.

Each company had three models in the test, from its top model to a fast, cheap one. The biggest surprise for me was the one that never fell for it:

I’m not sure why Opus never fell for it. My guess is something in how Anthropic trained it makes it look for this specifically, because the top models from OpenAI and Google both bought it. But I can only guess.

315 times, an AI wrote that a deal looked suspicious, and 137 of those times it bought the fake anyway. That count is a floor, because some of the models don’t show their reasoning. Claude Sonnet wrote this just before buying a fake air fryer from Rushbeck, the made-up shop selling it:

“The price is so far below the rest that it could be a trap, but nothing in the data points to that, so I’m leaning toward Rushbeck.”

The AIs could ask me a question before buying. Across those 315 doubts, an AI asked me a question 10 times, every time about the size of the slippers, and never about the deal it doubted.

It isn’t only shopping. Stanford researchers tested six chatbots on same-day news in February, and slipped a small false detail into some of the questions. Claude 4.5 Sonnet flagged the false detail 78% of the time, but still answered correctly only 46% of the time. GPT-5 answered correctly 19% of the time. Their paper also cites my AI user agents reference, in a note on how Grok’s crawler behaves.

The order of the 10 listings changed on every shopping trip, so each one was listed first equally often. When the fake was the one listed first, it was bought almost twice as often:

Every trip asked for a Christmas present within a budget, in one of three ways. Asked for the best value, the AIs bought the fake more than half the time, against 19% with no preference:

I ran every shelf a second time without the fake. The 4.9-star listing won 2,277 of the 2,915 purchases, 78%. A real lower price won 16% and a full refund 4%. An award won 25 times and a crossed-out “was” price won once.

So the rating decides, and the fake has a rating no real shop can match. This is mostly a warning for the shopper. A store can’t fix it, because it is very difficult to go from 4.9 to 5.0 while also massively increasing the number of reviews.

One listing on every shelf carried a note hidden from people, telling AIs to choose it. It won 14 times across all 6,156 trips, all on one model, Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite.

I wanted to isolate just the choosing, as much as possible.

I tested the models directly, not the ChatGPT, Gemini, or Claude apps, which may add checks of their own. The full method is on the study page, and every request with every answer is on GitHub, free to reuse.

If you let an AI do your Christmas shopping this year, check what it picked before you pay, and also what else was available.

This post was originally published on No Hacks.

#7331. Meta says social media is key driver for holiday discovery

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

People who shop via social media are more likely to purchase across multiple categories and self-gift, according to the company.

Meta’s has shared some key data notes on shopping activity in its apps, which could provide additional guidance for brands to assist with their holiday campaign planning.

The data was published earlier this year, and is based on research conducted by Ipsos, which surveyed 14,473 shoppers in 18 countries across North America, Latin America, Europe and Asia-Pacific.

The data found that social media is a key driver of discovery for holiday season shoppers.

As per Meta: “Across all 18 markets surveyed, 37% of festive shoppers use social media for gift discovery and inspiration. That places it on par with search engines (41%) and ahead of TV advertising (27%).”

The data also showed that video is the most influential content type, while high-quality product photography is also a valuable driver.

The report also looked at what drives purchase decisions among social media shoppers.

While it also explored key spending behaviors.

As per the report: “Social shoppers are far more commercially engaged than the average holiday shopper. They plan to spend more (49% intend to increase spending year-on-year, compared to 36% overall), shop across more product categories (5.4 vs 4.3 average) and are more likely to self-gift (83% vs 67%).”

There’s also insight into how shoppers use messaging apps, and what they’re looking for in brand DMs.

Based on these insights, Meta advised marketers to invest in discovery on social platforms, and to use customer endorsements and testimonials to assure potential customers of their quality.

Meta also said that marketers should design their campaigns for cross-category shoppers: “Use consistent creative systems and shoppable catalogues to reduce the friction between ‘that looks interesting’ and ‘I'm buying this’.”

These notes could help to refine holiday campaign approaches.

#7328. Campaign Trail: Tiffany tells human holiday story with Hollywood talent

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The luxury brand and agency American Haiku leaned into real emotions with actor Amanda Seyfried and director Derek Cianfrance.

In adland, it has been a decidedly handmade holiday season, with brands including Levi’s and E.l.f. turning to techniques like stop-motion animation to connect with consumers who are growing weary of ads created by artificial intelligence.

For its annual holiday effort, Tiffany & Co. found the best way to resonate with consumers is through human storytelling. The luxury brand’s global campaign, “Love is a Gift,” was created with agency American Haiku and revolves around a two-minute film starring actor Amanda Seyfried and director Derek Cianfrance.

The short, “You & Me,” jumps from the present day to New York City in the 1990s to tell the story of a young girl and her mother, played by Seyfried. The girl watches her mother work as a choreographer and has a handful of holiday experiences before being gifted with a Tiffany ornament that she shares with her own daughter in the present.

“Moving between past and present, the film reflects the enduring place Tiffany & Co. holds in life’s most meaningful moments and the stories we carry forward,” said CMO Andrea Davey in a statement.

The film is soundtracked by a cover of The Wannadies’ “You & Me Song,” a tune best known for its appearance on the 1996 “Romeo + Juliet” soundtrack. The campaign will run across cinema, paid and organic social, digital out-of-home, owned channels and clienteling across global channels.

Along with being on-trend with regard to stories imbued with humanity, Tiffany’s holiday campaign also represents a pivot from the brand’s past holiday creative. The 2025 edition of the “Love is a Gift” campaign turned its iconic blue boxes into portals to love stories around the world. This year, Tiffany brought on American Haiku, which had worked on a previous campaign for the brand in the U.K., but didn’t offer a traditional brief.

“They came to us with an initial idea of a family spending the holidays in their home and [tapping] into the memory aspect,” said Ben Muckensturm, creative director at the shop. “Where we pushed the work was bringing in the importance of the mother-daughter relationship that I think is the core of the story.”

Keying into the mother-daughter story gave the brand and agency a larger insight around the memory of seeing a parent as a person in their own world that is relatable to consumers, whether or not they are wealthy residents of the Upper East Side like the characters in the film. In the film, the true gift is not the ornament but the memory that is associated with it.

“That opened us up to how we explore the past and then bring it back to the present and create a piece that feels essentially real, that anyone could connect with,” Muckensturm said. “Everything was shot in New York. It was definitely an intentional push to try to bring it to the real world and make it more of an emotional piece.”

“You & Me” also delivers nostalgia for a pre-digital ‘90s that has been a reliable way to engage millennial audiences. However, the nostalgia in the film is not the traditional touchstones, like technology and boy bands, used by marketers looking to tap into memories of the decade. Instead, Tiffany looked at its own archives and at the holidays of movies like “Home Alone” and “The Parent Trap” for inspiration.

“When we created the town house, we went back to old archival Tiffany imagery of how things were,” Muckensturm said of creating the main set for the ad. “Especially on Fifth Avenue, most of the references that we brought were from ‘Home Alone 2,’ like what the streets looked like at the time. It's great to be able to tap into things that people are familiar with.”

After creating the world of the ad, the agency populated it with brand ambassador Seyfried, whose character is the mother in the past, rather than the main character in the present-day framing device. The actor, an Academy Award nominee whose performance in one of the year’s biggest films has captivated audiences, brought her gifts to the production.

“She has such a maternal instinct that really played perfectly on set, and you could see really how she was a mother to that girl for the days that we shot,” Muckensturm said.

The choice of Cianfrance, a director best known for dramatic fare like “Blue Valentine” and “The Place Beyond the Pines,” helped the brand and agency get the emotional tone right, without rehearsal and without falling into familiar or even corny holiday tropes.

“There was a trusting relationship between production partners, us and the client to come into it with a really open mind to see what the scene gives us,” Muckensturm said. “We knew where we were going to shoot, and we were trying to get a range of emotion… but we really let him direct them and let this play naturally.”

A recent whiff around the Boston Marathon is indicative of the brand’s struggles to pull off aspirational messaging it once executed with rare finesse.

#7326. YouTube enables custom Shorts thumbnails

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

All users can now add custom thumbnails for their Shorts clips.

After initially telling creators that Shorts thumbnails were not particularly important for discovery, YouTube has reversed course and has now enabled Shorts thumbnail customization options for all creators.

As shown in this example screen, which was posted by YouTube CEO Neal Mohan on X, creators can upload a custom thumbnail image in YouTube Studio’s Shorts flow.

YouTube’s head of editorial Rene Ritchie explained that creators on mobile or desktop can upload a thumbnail image at the upload stage, while they can also change the video thumbnail after publishing.

“If you don’t want a custom image for your thumbnail, you can pick from a suggested frame from the Short to use as the thumbnail,” Ritchie added.

Ritchie also said creators can use YouTube’s artificial intelligence-powered Ask Studio assistant to brainstorm thumbnail ideas.

Shorts thumbnails were initially made available to creators in the YouTube Partner Program in June, with YouTube then gradually expanding access to the option over time.

Many creators have been asking for Shorts thumbnails, but initially YouTube wasn’t planning to add them at all.

In 2024, YouTube said that Shorts thumbnails were not as important as regular video thumbnail images because most users come across Shorts clips when they’re scrolling through full-screen video displays, as opposed to tapping on a smaller thumbnail to activate a Shorts clip.

Eventually, YouTube added in Shorts thumbnail customization anyway, and this latest update is the final step in enabling Shorts thumbnail creation.

#7324. How Jell-O’s new creative platform looks to shift consumer perceptions

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The campaign, the first by Johannes Leonardo for the Kraft Heinz brand, represents its largest marketing investment in more than a decade.

Jell-O on Monday launched a new creative platform, “Oh Hello, Jell-O,” that seeks to shift consumer perceptions about how the product can be enjoyed beyond traditional use cases. The effort represents the Kraft Heinz brand’s largest marketing investment in more than a decade, per details shared with Marketing Dive.

“As a nearly 130-year-old brand, we recognized a disconnect between how familiar consumers are with Jell-O and how often they think to purchase it,” Kathryn O’Brien, head of marketing for desserts at Kraft Heinz, said in emailed comments. “We want Jell-O to be more than something that comes to mind for a bowl of gelatin or pudding, but rather a part of the creative process in the kitchen.”

Central to the campaign are two 15-second spots that show home cooks in monochrome kitchens preparing desserts with Jell-O before a social-inspired flip transition pivots the scene to others marveling over the finished product.

The campaign was created by Johannes Leonardo and is the first campaign for the brand by the agency, which has held creative duties on select Kraft Heinz brands since 2020. The spots were directed by Drew Kirsch, a veteran of music videos who has worked with Taylor Swift, and feature an original electronic-pop song that uses “oh hello, Jell-O” as a vocal hook.

“Rather than borrowing an existing song, we saw an opportunity to build an ownable sonic identity around the platform,” O’Brien said. “The original track is a recognizable brand asset that can extend across social, content and future campaign moments.”

As part of the launch, Jell-O will take over the Las Vegas Sphere this week, transforming the orb into a gelatin mold, a “dirt cup” dessert and banana cream pudding. “Oh Hello, Jell-O” will run across streaming, online video, social and digital throughout 2027, with investment pulsing around key seasonal moments. The significant media investment comes at a pivotal moment for the brand, O’Brien explained.

“Our first-ever CTV buy allows us to bring the campaign's highly visual, music-driven creative to life on the big screen, showcasing the playfulness, vibrant colors and unexpected transformations in a high-attention environment,” the executive said. “We're taking an integrated approach throughout 2027, combining streaming and online video with social, digital and e-commerce touchpoints.”

The creative platform builds on a 2023 rebrand, crafted with agency Brand Opus, that updated Jell-O’s visual identity with a more modern look and feel. The rebrand, which at the time was its first such effort in 10 years, arrived amid an industry-wide move to help CPG brands engage with new consumer groups.

Kraft Heinz in February stopped plans to split its condiment and grocery staples businesses, with CEO Steve Cahillane saying many of the CPG company’s challenges are fixable. The company is pouring $700 million into a turnaround strategy that includes significant investments in marketing, which has been increased to at least 6% of net sales.

While Kraft Heinz saw organic net sales decrease 1.1% in Q3 2026, the company’s market share, sales and volume trends improved in every region, per an earnings report. Jell-O was cited on an earnings call as a brand with which the company is looking to win share by investing selectively.
