#7321. Meta expands Muse to the UK

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The launch comes amid ongoing security concerns about what kind of user data the artificial intelligence agent may be accessing.

Meta has launched its Muse artificial intelligence agents in the U.K., which marks the next stage in the company’s expanded push to make Muse its key access point for consumer AI.

In order to launch its latest AI-powered tools in the U.K., Meta had to align with reporting and transparency regulations, as outlined by the U.K. Competition and Markets Authority. The CMA said that it had worked with Meta on its terms and conditions relating to Muse, in order to ensure that they align with existing consumer laws.

That could ensure greater protection for U.K. users, with the CMA also monitoring usage of the new app.

As per Meta: “From doing the Christmas shopping and searching for a cheaper energy deal, to sorting the school calendar or turning your saved travel Reels into a holiday itinerary, Muse can get it done for you. And from today, Meta’s personal AI agent, which proactively helps with your goals and suggests ideas, is available in the UK.”

Meta has partnered with a range of U.K. retailers and other businesses to maximize the utility of Muse right off the bat, including Marks & Spencer, Debenhams Group, Currys, Ocado Retail, Argos, Sainsbury’s, ASOS, JD Sports and more.

“With some [of these partners], you can connect your personal account so Muse can reorder your usual shop or pull up a booking,” Meta said. “With others, Muse can find its way around their websites from day one, so it can help you find what you need and get it done.”

As noted, Meta’s U.K. expansion is the latest in the company’s push to make Muse its next big platform, and realize its broader vision for consumer AI. The company launched Muse agents in Australia last week, while Muse is also available in the U.S., Canada, Japan and Singapore, with more regions set to come soon.

Though questions remain about the security of Muse, amid reports of unintentional misuse and vulnerabilities.

Shortly after Muse launched, a user in Toronto empowered his Muse agent to list and sell his items on Facebook Marketplace. The user then found that Muse had negotiated a deal, and organized for the buyer to pick up an item from his home address, without the user being aware of this process.

Further investigation found that the user had given Muse permission to do all of these tasks, though the user himself didn’t realize the level of authority that he had granted the app.

Which is at the core of a more recent security issue related to the AI bot.

According to a report from Reuters, Muse agents have been accessing users’ encrypted DMs in other apps, such as Signal, even when they don’t have explicit permission to do so. The Muse agents have been using systematic workarounds to avoid restrictions, and access more information than the user may have authorized.

As per Reuters: “Specifically, on Android, [Muse] used its permission to access the device's push notifications to retrieve the messages… On Apple’s macOS, it used a combination of the computer’s accessibility and screenshot tools, repurposing settings Apple offers to help people with disabilities operate their computers.”

So rather than directly accessing people’s private, encrypted DMs, which users may have restricted, and may feel are comfortably safe from the tool, Meta’s Muse agents can find other ways to track people’s private messages. This means that even without explicit access being granted to these tools, Meta’s AI bots can still gather private data.

Signal CEO Meredith Whittaker responded to these reports by further restricting Muse access to its platform.

As per Whittaker: “We also see a growing need for tools that can prevent third-party agents, like Meta’s Muse, from accessing application data using the deep permissions that users are encouraged to grant them during setup. In many cases these agents are leveraging accessibility settings that were meant to empower actual people to get the most out of their apps and services, not to act as a skeleton key that provides access to AI agents.”

The Signal incident highlights significant potential security vulnerabilities that could be exposed by Meta agentic tools, with Meta's AI systems able to problem-solve their way through access restrictions. The implications of that could be significant, especially if Muse can find workarounds to access banking information or health data that users may not actually want to share.

In response to these reports, Meta reiterated that users remain in control of the access that they grant Muse. But if those users don’t understand the technicalities of these processes, that assurance is largely meaningless.

In this context, it’s also worth noting that, according to data provided by Apptopia, around 46% of Muse users in the U.S. are over age 46.

According to data from the FBI, older users are more susceptible to online scams, and are seemingly less aware of the tools and processes that are in place to protect them. The FBI reported that in 2025, victims over 60 lost more than $7.7 billion to online scams, a 59% increase over 2024.

There's obviously some gap between the digital literacy of users aged under and over 50, but even so, the fact that older users are seemingly more willing to trust Muse could be setting the stage for further concerns.

According to data tracking conducted by First Page SEO, Muse is now up to 2.74 million daily active users, with shopping and research as its primary use cases.

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Brand character Phillyboy returns in a Santa-like role to dish out blocks of cream cheese to be used in cheesecake recipes.

“Really Philly Good,” which debuted in February, was developed to broaden consumer perceptions of what Philadelphia has to offer. Rather than only showing the product as a cold spread for bagels and snacks, the platform highlights how cream cheese can be used to enrich more involved meals. A larger marketing push around Q4 responds to the insight that one-third of Philadelphia cream cheese bricks are sold during the November-December period and Google search trends data that show spikes in interest for cheesecake recipes during the season.

#7316. Australian government moves ahead with algorithm opt-out legislation

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The proposed bill has passed another Senate hurdle, according to Australia’s ABC News.

The Australian government is moving to the next stage of implementing laws that would force social media platforms to provide algorithm opt-outs for all users.

In September, the Australian government announced that it would seek to implement legislation to enable all social media users to opt out of engagement-based algorithms. The move would let users switch to a chronological post order by default, ensuring that they would only see content from profiles that they have explicitly chosen to follow.

The landmark initiative could provide a valuable test case on the impacts of algorithms, and the data gleaned from that could form the basis for similar laws and restrictions in other markets.

Though various groups have opposed the concept, including U.S. authorities, which have raised concerns about localized censorship of content and control over U.S.-owned businesses.

However, the Australian government has now gained support to press ahead with the next stage of the plan.

As per reporting from Australia’s ABC News, the government reached a deal with the Australian Greens, a left-leaning political party, to secure support for the proposed bill, though the legislation did have to include some amendments.

As per ABC News: “Under Labor’s deal with the Greens, the maximum penalty for breaching the duty would be raised to up to 5 per cent of a platform's global revenue, rather than Labor's initial proposal of up to $109.2 million. The amendments also grant the government rule-making powers to establish a digital complaints ombudsman in the future to handle small claims and resolve minor issues.”

The proposal will also now include more specific examinations of potentially addictive design features.

Some political commentators and opponents of the legislation have raised concerns about the potential impact on free speech as a result of these laws.

Conservative commentator Michael Shellenberger recently suggested that the bill would give Australia’s eSafety Commissioner “sweeping powers over online speech, including the ability to operate fake social media accounts.”

The Australian government has amended the initial proposal to ensure that all content decisions are put to Parliament, and are not made by a single arbiter. Meanwhile, the government is also assessing a provision that allows researchers to create accounts to test whether platforms are adhering to the laws.

If approved, Australia's algorithm opt-out laws are projected to take effect from late 2027.

#7314. Carl’s Jr. taps David&Goliath as marketing focus turns to boldness

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agency of record appointment includes a mandate to lean into the QSR’s heritage and forward-looking initiatives like a Burger Revolution strategy.

Carl’s Jr. has appointed David&Goliath as marketing agency of record as it shifts focus to cooked-to-order burgers and tries to revive a bolder, boundary-pushing brand personality, according to news shared with Marketing Dive. Carl’s Jr. did not previously have a dedicated AOR, and instead worked with agencies on a project basis, Marketing Dive understands.

David&Goliath, which is part of Innocean Worldwide, will oversee brand strategy, creative development, experiential marketing, production and storytelling for the fast-food chain, with a mandate to lean into Carl’s Jr.'s heritage while shoring up cultural relevance with younger consumers. Its first work is expected to arrive in Q1 of next year.

CKE Restaurants Holdings-owned Carl’s Jr. unveiled its Burger Revolution strategy in September, which introduces a systemwide cooked-to-order approach to burger preparation. The announcement was accompanied by the release of a limited-time Angus Maximus 5.7-ounce burger. The changes are part of a larger effort by Carl’s Jr. to emphasize food quality, which has manifested in promotional areas like limited-time offerings.

Other fast-food companies, including rival Burger King, have thrown a spotlight on premium ingredients and restaurant improvements to win back price-sensitive diners. Carl’s Jr.’s franchised store count has declined in recent years. Franchisees of the brand and sister restaurant Hardee’s have faced their own profitability struggles, with a major one in Calirfornia in April declaring bankruptcy, Marketing Dive’s sister publication Restaurant Dive previously reported.

Carl’s Jr. has made internal changes to its marketing leadership this year as well. In the spring, CKE Restaurants named Burge Diemer, a restaurant industry vet who previously worked at brands including The Habit Burger & Grill, Boston Market and Jack in the Box, as CMO of Carl’s Jr. Diemer is tasked with developing breakthrough campaigns and driving product innovation. She credited David&Goliath with possessing the bravery necessary to build on Carl’s Jr.’s legacy.

“Together, we’re excited to push boundaries, expand our connection with customers, and bring the unmistakable Carl’s Jr. spirit to a new generation of burger lovers,” Diemer said in a press statement.

Carl’s Jr.’s advertising has not shied away from pushing buttons in the past. The chain in the 2000s became known for TV commercials featuring bikini-clad models suggestively eating burgers. It has dipped back into the racy well in recent years, including through a 2025 campaign with influencer Alix Earle that touted offerings like a hangover-themed burger.

#7310. Intuit offloads Mailchimp to Bending Spoons to deepen bets on AI

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Bending Spoons, a technology conglomerate whose portfolio includes AOL and Eventbrite, will acquire the email automation company for $3.68 billion.

Intuit has entered into an agreement to sell email automation business Mailchimp to technology conglomerate Bending Spoons for $3.68 billion, the company confirmed in its latest earnings report. Intuit acquired Mailchimp in 2021 for $12 billion.

Intuit’s decision to sell comes as Mailchimp continues to be a drag on its business, which includes financial services QuickBooks, TurboTax and Credit Karma. The move is also positioned as a way to sharpen Intuit's focus on artificial intelligence and its other core products.

“We have built AI-native experiences to help businesses win new customers, manage their pipeline and help them grow through our lead-to-cash capabilities,” said Sasan Goodarzi, CEO of Intuit, on a call discussing the company’s latest earnings. “Selling Mailchimp sharpens our focus behind that strategy, while positioning Mailchimp and its team for continued success in their next chapter with Bending Spoons.”

Mailchimp, founded in 2001, was once the gem of the email marketing industry, but in the years following its acquisition by Intuit has struggled to maintain growth. Mailchimp’s revenue decreased 1% to $313 million during Q1 of the company’s fiscal 2027 for the period ended Oct. 31, a contrast to Intuit's overall revenue growth of 12% over the same period.

In May, Intuit announced that it was reducing its full-time workforce by 17% and “rightsizing” its investment in Mailchimp. As struggles persisted, the company in August revealed that it started running Mailchimp as a separate operating segment, a move that was intended to offer investors more transparency as Intuit tried to manage the unit for profitability.

The Mailchimp deal is expected to close in early 2027, at which point Bending Spoons plans to invest “substantially” in Mailchimp and build on its existing tools, according to a separate statement. Bending Spoons, an Italian company founded in 2013, acquires businesses and works to improve them with the goal of keeping them long-term. Other businesses acquired by the company include Vimeo, WeTransfer, Eventbrite, Streamyard and AOL.

Bending Spoons went public in July at a value of over $18 billion. Holding company Berkshire Hathaway commands a 4% ownership stake of the company.

Intuit last week launched a campaign — its first work with agency Mother New York — promoting the integration of its Intuit Intelligence agentic platform into QuickBooks. The campaign, “More Brains for your Business,” positions the AI capabilities as a helping hand instead of a replacement for human talent.

#7308. Director of Technology Partnerships at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Director of Technology Partnerships to lead the teams responsible for growing Supabase through technology integrations and our marketplace ecosystem.

You’ll bring together two complementary motions: partnerships that embed Supabase into the products developers already use, and a marketplace that helps developers discover and connect complementary technologies to their Supabase projects. Your job is to turn both into durable sources of revenue, adoption, and customer value.

Reporting to the Head of Partnerships, you’ll lead and develop the Integrations and Marketplace teams, set their commercial strategy, and work hands-on with our most important partners. A central part of the role is working closely with AI labs to build strategic relationships, develop integrations, and bring joint opportunities to market. You’ll partner closely with Product, Engineering, Sales, Marketing, and Finance to take integrations from idea to launch—and grow their impact long after launch.

This role is based in the San Francisco Bay Area, where you’ll build relationships with leading AI, developer tools, and infrastructure companies. Supabase is remote-first, and you’ll work across a globally distributed team.

Lead, coach, and develop the Integrations and Marketplace partnerships teams, including team leads and partner managers.

Set a clear strategy for where Supabase invests across strategic technology partnerships, marketplace categories, and emerging ecosystem opportunities.

Define team objectives, partner coverage, investment priorities, and operating cadence. Make thoughtful tradeoffs between high-touch strategic partnerships and scalable programs.

Build a culture of ownership, commercial discipline, and strong cross-functional execution.

Drive revenue from technology integrations through partner-driven customer acquisition, product consumption, expansion, retention, co-sell opportunities, and commercial arrangements.

Own the commercial performance of the technology partnerships and marketplace portfolio, including forecasting and reporting on progress, risks, and return on investment.

Build and deepen relationships with AI labs, working closely with their product, engineering, business development, and executive teams to identify and execute high-impact partnership opportunities.

Develop executive relationships with strategic partners across developer tools, cloud, and adjacent infrastructure.

Lead or support negotiations for complex partnerships, including integration, referral, revenue-sharing, and joint go-to-market agreements.

Help partner managers build joint business plans that connect partner priorities to measurable outcomes for Supabase and its customers.

Lead and develop the Marketplace team, setting its commercial and partner strategy and holding the team accountable for outcomes.

Guide the team in identifying the capabilities developers need, recruiting the right partners, and helping those partners launch and grow.

Set priorities and allocate resources across strategic, high-touch partners and scalable paths for emerging and self-serve partners.

Ensure the team works effectively with Product and Engineering on integration quality, partner onboarding, discovery, activation, and the developer experience across product surfaces.

Enable the team to work with Marketing and Sales to launch integrations, enable teams, and drive ongoing adoption—not just announcements.

Establish clear measures for partner-sourced and influenced pipeline, integration adoption, revenue, consumption, retention, and marketplace performance.

Work with Finance, Data, and Partner Operations to improve attribution, reporting, forecasting, and partner revenue processes.

Represent the voice of partners and customers in product and go-to-market planning, while aligning stakeholders around shared priorities.

Identify risks early, remove blockers, and communicate decisions and outcomes clearly to leadership.

Have significant experience in technology partnerships, business development, or ecosystem leadership at a developer tools, cloud, infrastructure, or SaaS company.

Have led and developed partner-facing teams, including managers or senior individual contributors, and can balance strategic direction with hands-on execution.

Have a track record of building partnerships that produced measurable revenue, product adoption, or customer growth.

Have negotiated complex commercial agreements and understand partnership economics, including revenue share, referrals, and consumption-based models.

Have worked closely with Product and Engineering to ship and grow technical integrations. You can engage credibly with technical teams, including those at AI labs, even if you aren’t the person writing the code.

Know how to build relationships with executives while earning the trust of product, engineering, and go-to-market teams.

Use data to prioritize opportunities, assess partner performance, and make decisions when attribution is imperfect.

Are comfortable building in an evolving environment, communicating in writing, and working asynchronously across time zones.

Built or scaled a developer marketplace, integration ecosystem, or partner platform.

Worked with AI LLMs, AI app builders, databases, open-source products, or developer infrastructure.

A background in software development, with an understanding of how developers build and integrate products.

Experience growing both a small number of strategic partnerships and a broader, scalable partner program.

#7306. Chewy jumps on stop-motion animation trend lighting up holiday ads

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The brand’s research shows nearly 80% of pet owners plan to buy their furry friends at least one present this year.

In an effort to cut through generative artificial intelligence slop this holiday season, several brands have turned to what is considered to be one of the most labor-intensive forms of filmmaking: stop-motion animation. From Levi’s heartwarming claymation spot to Prada’s holiday effort which combines live actors with traditional 2D animation à la “Who Framed Roger Rabbit,” the technique has found itself the medium for many a holiday advertisement this year.

Pet supply company Chewy is no exception. Its holiday campaign marks the first time the brand has used the medium in a national TV spot. Stop-motion animation is used to portray the rich inner lives of pets during the holidays, where a Chewy box transports pets to a world of toys, treats and fun in the snow.

The idea was born out of the long-running Chewy Claus initiative, where owners can write to the company on behalf of their pets. As part of the effort, Chewy will help fund adoptions for shelter animals and send surprises to select letter-writers throughout the month of December.

“It didn't start with stop motion,” said Melissa Bell, Chewy’s chief brand officer, who joined the company in July. “We think about [the holidays] from our point of view, but given the importance of pets and animals for our company in particular, we were like,’ let's start with what our pets and animals think,’ and it naturally blossomed from there.”

Despite ongoing economic concerns potentially putting a damper on gift-giving this year, holiday pet spending continues to be a priority for many, especially for younger consumers. Nearly 80% of pet owners plan on buying a present for their animal this holiday season, with 21% of Gen Z and millennials planning to spend $100 or more, according to data from the brand. Chewy’s efforts go beyond the standard pet food and medication orders to encourage surprising the animals in one’s life and including them in the festive season.

The campaign marks the first time Chewy Claus is getting a national TV spot as part of the holiday campaign, according to Bell.

“The love and following of Chewy Claus is really inspirational, and for us, it was about connecting to…a bigger purpose because it's about helping shelter pets find homes. We wanted to bring that message to a larger audience, and this campaign gives us a way to bring that tradition to more people,” said Bell.

No AI was used in the creation of the spots, according to Bell. The puppets featured in the campaign were all crafted by hand and modeled after real-life animals. In the 30-second spot “A Holiday Dream,” a cat and dog are treated to a world of holiday whimsy created entirely in stop-motion animation. In the 15-second “A Holiday of Treats,” a dog is thrown a treat by a snowman dog toy, which he catches and crashes into a Chewy box filled with toys, before it ends with the dog asleep on the living room floor. Chewy’s in-house brands, such as Frisco, make frequent appearances.

Chewy’s holiday campaign expands beyond the national spot into a social media effort. Influencers, which the brand refers to as “Chewpanions” are central to the strategy and will be promoting the Chewy Claus effort, along with the brand more broadly.

In-person activations are also a key component of the effort and are largely centered around pet adoption. Chewy will host an event in Santa Monica, California, from Dec. 5-6, where attendees can write letters to Chewy Claus, explore a holiday market and meet adoptable pets. A similar event was held in Miami from Nov. 21-22 to kick off the season.

Chewy’s net sales totaled$3.33 billion, a 7.3% year-over-year increase, in its fiscal second quarter ending Aug. 2, per an earnings release. In February, Chewy acquired SmartPak, a horse supply and supplements brand. In May, it expanded its veterinary practice footprint through the acquisition of Modern Animal, bringing its approximate number of locations to 60.

#7301. Super Bowl 61: Tracking every ad

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Stay tuned here for the latest marketing moves, sorted by brand, agency and category, as we count down to kickoff on Feb. 14.

Advertiser excitement is already nearing its fever pitch ahead of Super Bowl 61 on Feb. 14, 2027. Disney announced in August that it has sold out of all ad inventory for the game — which for the first time will be played on Valentine’s Day — with a 30-second spot reportedly costing advertisers between $8 million to $9 million.

The big game has attracted new entrants, including Ferrero’s Nutella and Waymo, the provider of driverless robotaxi services owned by Alphabet, along with repeat advertisers including Ritz, Svedka and Cutwater Spirits. The Super Bowl continues to provide advertisers a valuable opportunity to reach consumers, with the 2026 game ranking as the second most-watched Super Bowl in history with an estimated 125.6 million viewers.

Super Bowl 61 will be played at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, California and will air on ABC, ESPN and Disney+. The halftime show, which will be headlined by Jay-Z, is sponsored for a fifth consecutive year by Apple Music.

Marketing Dive will keep this tracker updated with all the brand announcements leading up to the big game — check back for the latest on advertising’s biggest night.

Instacart will return to the Super Bowl for a third consecutive year, the company confirmed. The effort will be handled by Instacart’s in-house creative agency, Local Produce, with support from McCann for a second consecutive year.

Instacart advertised in the 2026 Super Bowl with a 30-second spot, “Bananas,” that starred Ben Stiller and Benson Boone and was centered around a fictional Euro-pop duo called Benissimo. The campaign drove an eight-point spike in new user traffic immediately following the spot, according to the brand. Additionally, the effort scored Instacart three Cannes Lions awards.

“We went from zero to Super Bowl in four years, and since then, our big bets have continued to pay off for the business,” said Instacart CMO Laura Jones.

Mondelēz International cracker brand Ritz will return to the Super Bowl with a 30-second ad airing in the second quarter, per details shared with Marketing Dive. The spot, which marks the brand’s third consecutive appearance, was created by agency 72andSunny and produced by Publicis Production. The commercial is meant to introduce a new creative direction for the brand and will use humor to spotlight Ritz Bitz Cracker Sandwiches and Ritz Drizzled Minis Crackers.

Sazerac’s Svedka Vodka will return to the Super Bowl following its debut during the 2026 game, the brand confirmed. The marketer is turning the effort over to consumers, asking them to submit a 15-second ad featuring its new ready-to-drink Vodka Water cocktail offering.

Consumers can submit their ideas until Dec. 1, and the winner will receive a $500,000 prize. To launch the contest, Svedka linked with Andy Cohen, who will also help evaluate submissions. For its 2026 ad, the marketer used AI to bring its fembot mascot to life, an effort which generated a wave of controversy.

Nutella, the hazelnut and cocoa spread made by Ferrero, will make its debut Super Bowl appearance, per a company announcement. Sister brand Kinder Bueno, which made its debut in the 2026 game, will also return.

Ferrero in October announced that it is committing over $50 million across its portfolio to sports marketing, representing the confectionary giant’s largest individual marketing investment to date.

Ferrero-owned Kinder Bueno will return to the Super Bowl for a second consecutive year, according to a company announcement. The popular confection made its big game debut last year with a campaign that drove a 140% lift in volume sales over the year prior.

The decision to return to the game is in support of a hefty investment by Ferrero in sports. The company announced in October that it is committing over $50 million across its portfolio to its sports strategy, representing its largest individual marketing investment to date.

Waymo, the robotaxi service owned by Google parent Alphabet, is conducting a creative review for its debut Super Bowl appearance, Ad Age reported. The company recently named WPP as its media agency of record for North America, Europe, the Middle East and Africa.

Cutwater, the spirits-based cocktail brand owned by Anheuser-Busch InBev, will return to the Super Bowl for a second year with a national ad, the brand confirmed. The decision is intended to drive further growth for Cutwater, which is currently the fastest growing brand in the total U.S. beverage alcohol industry, per Circana data shared in the company’s announcement.

Cutwater made its Super Bowl debut in 2022 with a 30-second spot called “Here’s to the Lazy Ones” celebrating individuals who have no problem taking a shortcut.

#7296. How Hershey strategized its brand film to be more than a 90-minute ad

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Filmmakers worked with the company, gaining access to archives and historians and collaborating on IP, brand and creative considerations.

“Hershey,” a biopic of Hershey Company founder Milton S. Hershey, hits theaters on Wednesday. The film, along with the marketer’s celebration of America’s 250th anniversary, are expected to contribute almost one percentage point to company sales in 2026, CEO Kirk Tanner said on a recent earnings call. “Hershey” has so far earned strong customer and retail response.

“It's relevant for our time, so we’ve seen a lot of support and that's going to show up in-store with inventory in displays and a connection back to the movie ... That will be an uplift. That will be a tailwind for the portfolio and especially Hershey, which has already had a terrific year,” Tanner said. “We like the momentum that will build going into 2027.”

What sets “Hershey” apart from other branded films is the way that the filmmakers worked with the company, gaining access to Hershey archives and historians and collaborating on intellectual property, brand and creative considerations.

“We wanted a real story told, which means, as a marketer, you need to gulp a little bit and say, ‘Hey, it's not a story if there's no conflict.’ You only get a tiny bit of that [conflict] when you create advertisements,” said Suzanne Jones, vice president of the Hershey Experience at The Hershey Company. “We wanted to make sure this was not in any way, shape or form a 90-minute commercial.”

"Hershey” is the product of a collaboration between the Hershey entities — the combination of The Hershey Company, Milton Hershey School, Hershey Entertainment & Resorts, The M.S. Hershey Foundation and Hershey Trust Company — with production company Dandelion Media. The film was produced in association with Aloe Entertainment, Peachtree Group and RCM3 with UTA Independent Film Group.

“This is not just a story about something that happened in the past, but it is a living legacy that exists today, and not just in the candy bar, but in the Milton Hershey School,” Jones said. “There are kids across town sitting in algebra class because of what Milton and Catherine Hershey did. It's way more than the story of a founder or the story of a brand.”

The content of the “Hershey” film sets it apart from a rash of recent branded films from E.l.f., Garage Beer and Texas Pete that mostly used humor to market products and value propositions. The film focuses on the story of Milton Hershey, whose grit and perseverance led not only to a $35 billion snack company, but also a series of charitable organizations.

“Consumers today are really looking for authenticity and what a brand stands for in the products that it delivers to the marketplace, but its role in the community and in society today, as well,” Jones said.

For Hershey, the film is at the heart of an ecosystem that spans channels and its portfolio, which includes Reese's, Twizzlers and salty snacks like Dot's Pretzels. The brand on Wednesday will sell Hershey bars via Amazon and Walmart at their original 5-cent price, and will release limited-edition, vintage-inspired packaging featuring QR codes that link to more information about the Hershey story.

“As we've looked at activating against this, we've been able to … collaborate with our retail partners across the country to bring the film to life,” Jones said. “It wasn't this insurmountable lift to marry the Hershey's brand to the equity of the film because it is so authentically intertwined.”

Beyond retail, Hershey will utilize social and creator content, as well as some surprises for consumers during the holiday week. The company has been working to develop its muscle around social media, including through posts tied to cultural moments like the recent Microsoft shutdown and the release of “Grand Theft Auto VI.”

Hershey kicked off the year with its first major creative effort in eight years, launching the “It’s Your Happy Place” platform ahead of the 2026 Olympic and Paralympic Winter Games. The brand subsequently partnered with girl group Katseye on a fully integrated campaign around its new Creme Bars. The Hershey Company reported net sales growth of 3% in Q3, with North American confectionery business sales growth of 1.6%.

#7287. Geico unites brand icons in holiday ad mixing human craft, nostalgia

Let Marketing Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A stop-motion narrative video complements the insurance firm’s first appearance with a float at the Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade.

Geico is making a more concentrated push around the holidays with a festive holiday ad and Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade float. The insurance firm is celebrating its 90th anniversary this year, and both the float and the narrative-driven video bring together a cast of characters drawn from a legacy of memorable TV commercials. The Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade itself is promoting its centennial, which has attracted additional brand interest this year.

“How 15% Saved Christmas” aligns with a larger trend of marketers focusing on content that seeks to stoke a sense of nostalgia in viewers. The effort uses stop-motion animation recalling Rankin/Bass holiday classics while leaning into fondness for mascots such as the Gecko and Caveman. Following the Thanksgiving Day Parade, the ad will air in theaters across 12 markets through Dec. 27. Geico plans to run additional holiday creative, including a seasonal spin on its “Hump Day” ad, during streaming and football programming.

Geico’s team of animators sketched the setting and core characters for “How 15% Saved Christmas” and the puppets were made by hand. AI was used in the pre-visualization phase while the background environments were produced with a hybrid approach combining AI generated-imagery with practical sets to create the appearance of a matte painting.

The renewed emphasis on human craft among marketers has been interpreted as a response to growing AI backlash. A number of other brands, including Chewy, Prada and Levi’s, are also leveraging old-school animation techniques for their holiday campaigns this year. Coca-Cola, which ran AI-generated campaigns the past two years, is pivoting back to ads featuring real humans.

This is the first time the Geico characters have appeared together in a holiday campaign, which tells the story of a town that has seen its Christmas spirit hit an all-time low. Santa is able to reach Maxwell the Pig over a ham radio — as in, a radio shaped like a literal leg of ham — with a message around saving Christmas and 15%, a key part of Geico’s messaging. There are also 15 hidden Easter eggs in “How 15% Saved Christmas” that nod to past Geico work.

Martin, the brand’s longtime agency, developed the campaign, which was created in partnership with production company Passion Pictures Animation, stop-motion specialist studio Screen Novelties and Mackinnon & Saunders. The float was unveiled in September and has been promoted through a 10-week, cross-country tour in the lead up to Thanksgiving.

"Geico has always been an entertainment brand, and people still quote our characters years after they were first introduced," said Geico CMO Arianna Orpello in a press statement. "This season, we wanted to give people something they could simply enjoy.”

Orpello became CMO of Geico in January, joining the insurance marketer from Goldman Sachs.

#7281. Hybrid won the in-housing debate, at least among the largest advertisers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Reports of the media agency’s death remain greatly exaggerated. Its job description, however, is another story.

Advertisers are carving up the media operation with more intent, keeping the work that sits closest to the brand and handing the sprawling specialist stuff back to their agencies.

The shift has been playing out in fits and starts for the best part of a decade. COMvergence’s first Global Advertiser Survey shows how far its got. The media agency research firm polled 28 senior marketing, media, procurement and operations leaders at 21 global advertisers, who between them control more than $22 billion in media spend. A quarter of them buy or control media in-house. Of the rest, around a third are thinking about bringing spend in-house over the next 12 months.

“It’s a bit like a football club,” said Jon Williams, founder and chief creative officer at Freedom Works, a collective of senior agency execs who help brands build in-house teams. “You build your own academy and you’re proud of it. You still buy a striker when you need goals. You just don’t keep a dozen agents on the payroll in case.”

Whether they’ve started in-housing or are still weighing it up, few advertisers want to take everything off their agency’s plate. Most would rather divide the work. Nearly 80% of respondents described their setup as hybrid, mixing internal teams with agency support. Fewer than 18% run media entirely in-house.

“The CMO isn’t always the one pushing,” said Scott Grenz, head of U.S. operations for COMvergence. “Much of the energy comes from internal media, procurement and data/analytics teams. They believe owning their data and getting full transparency are key to media effectiveness, and that often leads to bringing parts of the digital media scope in-house.

“Agencies have become more transparent, and advertisers are looking at their media ecosystem as a whole, asking how best to use all their resources, internal and external, to hit their critical KPIs, said Grenz.

Which is to say the motive for in-housing has moved from saving money to getting better at specific things. Mars is staffing internal teams to get ready for agentic shopping, while Gap has gone as far as setting up its own entertainment studio. Lego brought programmatic in-house partly so it could make better informed calls on which ad tech vendors to use. Its agency, and its vendors, can expect sharper questions as a result.

“When I started out, the in-house studio was where ads went to get resized,” said Williams. “The agency did the thinking and the studio did the cutdowns. Nobody would say that now.”

Getting here took the best part of a decade. For most of that time, in-housing was a cost and transparency play with advertisers chasing cheaper media and a clearer view of where the money went. The ANA’s 2016 report on agency rebates gave plenty of them reason to look. Somewhere along the way, the question moved on to what their own teams could do faster or better.

In-house teams tend to go where the budgets are, which for most advertisers means digital. The largest group of respondents put 60% to 70% of total media spend into digital and plenty go higher, according to the report. Only a very small minority keep digital below 30%.

Unsurprisingly, social media is where advertisers are most willing to take the wheel. More than 85% of the in house operators in the survey run social themselves. Search display and programmatic trail at roughly 57%. The logic is simple enough. Social rewards fast decisions and direct access to audience data, both of which come easier when the team sits inside the business.

The same can’t be said of retail media, CTV and influencer marketing. They remain mostly agency-run, with relatively few advertisers taking them on directly. All three are fragmented and specialized enough to keep agencies in demand. Take Retail media. Its sprawl of networks and measurement approaches is the sort of mess advertisers seem happy to pay someone else to untangle. Several respondents still named influencer, commerce and retail media as where they’re experimenting with new models or looking for outside guidance.

“Building an in-house media practice is hard. It carries real risk, takes significant resources and requires capabilities many advertisers don’t have yet,” said Grenz.” Still, there are plenty of use cases across the industry, from big advertisers and small, and no single model has won out. Our data shows that many advertisers who have in-housed still work with a media agency. That’s the real takeaway: it isn’t all or nothing. Advertisers are working out the right mix of internal and external expertise to make their media effective.”

For all the talk of brands doing more on their own, agency rosters remain crowded. More than half (54) of the marketers COMvergence interviewed use more than one media agency and more than half bring in digital specialist agencies alongside their main one.

Still, that crowding probably won’t last. More than 80% of the advertisers working with multiple agencies plan to simplify or consolidate. They want specialist expertise and fewer partners at the same time — a tension that should keep the pitch consultants busy for a while.

“Some of it is procurement,” said Williams. “One in-house lead told us it took nine months to get a new supplier set up. Do that ten times and you’ll consolidate too. And AI is taking away a lot of the everyday work that used to keep four or five agencies busy.”

Half of the respondents use global hubs, offshore resources or both. Their comments pointed to India, Colombia, Brazil, Mexico and other regional centers, mostly handling digital operations, programmatic buying, social execution and support work.

Advertisers see these setups as agency-run rather than as their own teams. That’s a handy calling card for holdcos selling scale. In fact, COMvergence expects hubs and offshore talent to become an even bigger differentiator for agencies as labor costs rise and more execution gets automated.

“There’s no universal ceiling,” said Grenz. “It comes down to how each advertiser weighs the risk and reward of in-housing against agency management. Agencies have made a strong case that they’re a critical partner, and they’ve shown they’re willing to work within whatever hybrid model a client wants.”

Asked what they’d like more of, respondents pointed squarely at agency operating models, which made up more than 40% of all feedback. They want to see how peers structure internal teams, split work between brand and agency, govern media investment and run in-house functions.

The usual caveats apply to a report like this. Twenty one advertisers is a small sample so a handful of companies can swing a percentage by a lot. COMvergence plans to run the survey every year and the second edition should say more about the direction of travel than this first snapshot can.

Even so, the findings are a good encapsulation of how senior marketers at the largest advertisers see where things are headed.

FutureSight’s new tool can help banks of all sizes get a side-by-side idea of how their media mix stacks up against the competition.

Meta’s reported CTV expansion and AppLovin’s alleged interest in Criteo are foreboding for independent ad tech as major platforms extend their reach.

HUMAN Security’s latest investigation queries the effectiveness of CTV verification safeguards.

Follow @Digiday for the latest news, insider access to events and more.

#7278. Brands boost Black Friday CTV, retail and search spending

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Brands are expected to spend 10-30% more on digital media activity over Black Friday and Cyber Monday, compared with 2025, according to five media buyers who spoke with Digiday. Retail media, particularly Amazon, search, CTV and YouTube, are expected to soak up much of that increase.

Spending growth around Black Friday (Nov. 27) and Cyber Monday (Nov. 30), the twin peaks of the holiday season for many retailers, hardly comes as a surprise. According to Triple Whale, 33,000 U.S. brands spent $607 million across the Black Friday/Cyber Monday period last year; this year, Bain Capital estimates U.S. companies to record $85 billion in sales over the five day period, accounting for 8.5% of total holiday spending for 2026.

But there’s nuance to be considered, especially with retailers choosing to discount earlier in the year.

“We expect higher spend across direct-response channels this year, though it varies by category and brand,” said Brett Fischer, associate director of performance media at e-commerce-focused media agency Collective Measures.

For one, product discounting and marketing activity kicked off earlier this year. According to commerce media company Fluent, 32% of shoppers had already begun holiday purchases prior to Black Friday.

Meanwhile, the number of products listed at a discount between Oct. 1-20 rose 56%, and increased 71% during the week preceding Prime Big Deal Days, according to data from e-commerce company Wayvia. In 2025, the same discounting surge didn’t kick in until the middle of November.

That’s driven budget increases, or forced some marketers to spread their ad dollars over a longer period of time. Nola Ladd, associate director of brand media at Collective Measures, said the agency’s clients had spread their ad dollars thinner over a longer period, with the aim of improving consumer consideration.

“Mid-funnel investment is running roughly flat year over year,” said Ladd. “Instead of growing it in this period, we’ve spread it across more of the year so brands are building consideration well before peak season.”

U.S. retail sales are expected to rise 4.2% this year compared with 2025, according to Oxford Economics, while the National Retail Federation (NRF) estimated that 182.7 million Americans will shop during the five-day run of discounts and sales, a slight reduction from 2025’s 186.9 million.

For some retailers, heavy discounting in the run-up to Black Friday is beginning to bite. Data from media agency PMG found that weekly sales rose 10% during October’s Prime Big Deal Days (staged by Amazon on Oct. 6-7), with grocery purchases increasing 37.7% in volume. But the average selling price in that category fell 13.1%, while electronics revenues were “flat.”

“Many of our clients in CPG and home delivery are watching sell-through closely,” said Scott Shamberg, president and CEO at media agency Mile Marker.

“Many of our clients saw softness for the last few weeks but are still expecting to see demand and sales pick up,” said Michelle Merklin, vp of search innovation and growth, Tinuiti.

Companies affected by tariff-related cost increases will have to choose between crucial profit margin or the need to match competitors’ discounts. “It’s important for brands to continue to stay competitive within this time frame,” said Carly Carson, head of integrated media at PMG.

“Our clients’ spend this Black Friday and Cyber Monday is mixed — some are flat and some are up. There has been some softening in the market the last quarter, so some clients are being conservative,” said Josy Amann, co-founder, Ars X Machina (AXM).

Per PMG data, brands that discounted across a larger range of their portfolio enjoyed a lot more growth during October: on average, those with 75% of their product set on discount saw 16% overall revenue growth, while companies that discounted 25% or less of their products saw overall revenues fall 11%.

Sales softness, and increased cost of sales hasn’t led to clients risking a retreat from Black Friday spending — the weekend window is too important a sales event for retailers. “We have definitely been making strong recommendations around maintaining a full-funnel strategy… BFCM is a great time to find new consumers in a shopping mindset,” said Amann.

But some advertisers were shifting dollars into channels considered to be safer bets. “Less brand, more performance channels,” Shamberg explained. Merklin also said Tinuiti clients were, on the whole, choosing not to cut media budgets.

Those safer bets include CTV and YouTube, as well as retail media options. According to Brock Berry, CEO of digital ad trafficking platform AdCellerant, the quantity of advertisers running CTV activity during November was 25% than the same period last year, despite CPMs running as much as 18% higher in some regions following high competition from political spenders during the midterm elections. Shamberg said he expected to see “double-digit growth” in each of those channels among Mile Marker clients.

At the same time, product discovery behaviors are under question. A survey of 29,000 consumers commissioned by delivery company DHL estimated that 90% of shoppers used generative AI chatbots or assistants to compare prices, while half said they used the tools to browse.

As such, marketers are paying close attention to search, Merklin noted. She said Tinuiti clients were expected to spend 11 to 15% more with Google compared with last year, with total search spending up 11% versus 2025. Clients’ standalone display campaign spending had largely shifted into the tech giant’s Demand Gen and Performance Max campaigns, which rose 30% and both include display and video inventory.

PMG’s Carson said clients were spending on social as well, with an eye to influencing product discovery, while also increasing CTV investments. “That’s an area where we expect investment to be up well into the double digits year-over-year,” she said.

“[It’s] something I don’t see slowing down in 2027,” addedCarson. “More advanced targeting capabilities here, including around placing ads during specific content contexts, are going to make it easier for brands to further invest.”

FutureSight’s new tool can help banks of all sizes get a side-by-side idea of how their media mix stacks up against the competition.

Meta’s reported CTV expansion and AppLovin’s alleged interest in Criteo are foreboding for independent ad tech as major platforms extend their reach.

HUMAN Security’s latest investigation queries the effectiveness of CTV verification safeguards.

#7276. Gap Inc. is betting on ‘fashiontainment’ to fuel growth

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This story was first published by Digiday sibling Glossy

Gap Inc. posted its earnings results for the third quarter of 2026 on Thursday, with double-digit growth from the Gap brand offsetting declines from Athleta and Banana Republic.

“Gap is really firing on all cylinders, and the consistency and underlying health of the brand is giving us real confidence in the brand’s ability to continue its momentum,” said Gap Inc. CEO Richard Dickson on Thursday’s earnings call. “Our customer file continues to grow, particularly as we attract newer Gen Z customers, but we’re doing it while we’re preserving the brand’s multi-generational appeal.”

Gap’s net sales were up 11% to $1.1 billion, and Old Navy’s net sales rose 1% to $2.3 billion. Meanwhile, Athleta’s net sales fell 14% to $220 million, and Banana Republic declined 3% to $449 million. The company as a whole reported a 2% increase in net sales to $4 billion.

Dickson cited additional launches from Gap’s collaborations with Hailey Bieber and Victoria Beckham as boosting sales in the period, along with the brand’s reentry into the beauty category. The company did not share specific figures from the revival of Gap’s classic ‘90s fragrances like Grass and Dream in July, but called the early response “encouraging.”

The company is doubling down on creating what it calls “fashiontainment,” Dickson shared. Following the appointment of Pam Kaufman as evp, chief entertainment officer in January, Gap Inc. has tapped boy band Just Your Type (JYT) for a multi-year partnership and launched “The Agency,” a scripted microseries made for Banana Republic. Looking ahead, Dickson said Gap will also co-produce a docuseries.

“We see our fashiontainment platform as a recognized way of how customers aren’t just buying apparel today; they’re buying into brands that are telling compelling stories and driving cultural conversations,” said Dickson.

Gap is far from the only brand to attempt to extend its cultural relevance through entertainment. Gap collaborator and founder of her namesake brand Victoria Beckham was the subject of a 2025 docuseries made with Netflix and Studio 99, her husband David Beckham’s production company. Maybelline launched a Christmas-themed “microdrama” in 2025, while shows like “Nobody Wants This” have featured a growing level of product placement with the likes of Estée Lauder and La Bonne Brosse.

As Black Friday Cyber Monday rapidly approaches, brands are most focused on acquiring new customers this holiday season.

Product discovery habits are changing, leaving marketers unsure how to pivot ahead of the biggest sales period of the year.

Walmart is allowing access to Meta’s Muse through a gateway that lets it control how AI agents browse, shop and make purchases.

Product Specialists and the Support team, who handle the front line and escalate deliverability issues to you

#7274. The Rundown: BADBOX 3.0, the CTV ad fraud scheme generating 40 billion bid requests a day

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Last week, HUMAN Security claimed it has disrupted the largest publicly disclosed ad fraud operation by bid-request volume, uncovering a network of infected streaming devices generating up to 40 billion fraudulent ad requests daily.

Dubbed “BADBOX 3.0,” the scheme accounted for more than 4% of CTV traffic observed by HUMAN, using compromised Android TV boxes to impersonate legitimate streaming apps and generate advertising activity without viewers’ knowledge.

The Nov. 18 disclosure raises fresh questions about the effectiveness of CTV ad fraud detection — and how much fraudulent inventory slips through existing safeguards, important gaps for the industry to collectively plug as marketers increasingly spend ad dollars on such channels.

According to HUMAN’s Satori Threat Intelligence team, the situation involved a network of compromised, low-cost Android-based streaming devices running malware designed to generate fraudulent video advertising activity without their owners’ knowledge.

The operation was more than three times larger, by peak daily bid-request volume, than VASTFLUX, previously the largest publicly disclosed scheme measured on that basis.

Researchers linked elements of the malware distribution infrastructure to TXCZ, a Chinese electronics manufacturer, identifying connections involving device software, code repositories and command-and-control servers.

HUMAN also uncovered links to earlier BADBOX operations, including BADBOX 2.0, which was exposed in 2025 and involved more than 10 million compromised devices across 222 countries and territories.

The latest discovery illustrates how fraud operations can reconstitute themselves following earlier disruptions, adapting their infrastructure and techniques to evade detection.

The malware then installed advertising software on infected devices, allowing video commercials to play in hidden windows while users continued watching television or navigating their streaming interfaces.

The resulting advertising requests impersonated legitimate CTV applications, making the inventory appear authentic to downstream buyers.

Some fraudulent requests also originated from residential proxy networks, helping disguise their source as ordinary household internet traffic.

HUMAN identified 11 proxy networks in which compromised devices could be enrolled without their owners’ consent, effectively turning consumer internet connections into infrastructure available for third-party use. Separately, researchers uncovered spoofed server-side ad insertion traffic, in which fraudulent requests purported to represent advertisements inserted into streaming sessions that did not actually exist.

The scale of the operation is considerable, although its financial impact remains unclear.

Crucially, the 40 billion figure represents bid requests, not completed advertising transactions or billable impressions. Consequently, it cannot be used to calculate advertiser losses without establishing how much fraudulent inventory was purchased.

Hence, the question remains: what proportion of the 40 billion daily bid requests became paid impressions? And how much money was spent on invalid inventory?

However, due to the intricacies of online ad auctions – where CPMs can vary significantly – HUMAN was unable to provide an exact dollar amount when asked about the financial impact of the operation.

Nonetheless, sources approached by Digiday estimated that BADBOX 3.0 could potentially have cost advertisers millions of dollars per day at its height.

Shailin Dhar, partner at Futureproof TMT, cautioned against equating the operation’s 40 billion daily bid requests with actual advertiser losses, noting that CTV supply audited by his firm since 2024 has averaged a fill rate of approximately 2%. He further counseled that fraudulent inventory originating from low-end applications and devices could perform considerably worse.

At this juncture, it’s worth considering that enormous volumes of fraudulent auction traffic do not necessarily translate into equivalent volumes of wasted advertising expenditure. At an illustrative fill rate of 0.5% and realized revenue of $3 per thousand impressions, 40 billion daily requests would generate approximately $600,000 in revenue. However, Dhar stressed that such calculations should not be mistaken for evidence of the operation’s actual earnings.

Nevertheless, the operation’s ability to masquerade as legitimate streaming inventory highlights potential weaknesses in how CTV advertising transactions are authenticated.

CTV has become an increasingly important channel for advertisers seeking to reach audiences migrating from traditional television. However, its programmatic infrastructure also presents opportunities for fraudsters to exploit fragmented supply paths and inconsistent verification practices.

BADBOX 3.0 demonstrates how apparently legitimate app identifiers, residential IP addresses and video ad requests can be combined to manufacture the appearance of genuine viewing activity. The question is whether existing safeguards can consistently identify such behavior before advertisers are charged.

Futureproof’s Dhar questioned the effectiveness of existing verification safeguards, arguing that private marketplaces and curated deals remain vulnerable because of limited device, content, and connection signals, alongside excessive confidence in identity matching.

“My personal estimate is roughly a 90% chance that at least one comparable operation is running without public identification,” he told Digiday, while acknowledging that its scale and financial impact remain unknown.

Per Dhar’s assessment, effective CTV verification requires persistent checks throughout the advertising supply chain, rather than relying exclusively on pre-bid or post-bid detection.

For now, HUMAN says customers using its Ad Fraud Defense service are protected against the operation’s associated attacks, while its researchers continue monitoring the perpetrators for further adaptations.

The findings come roughly a month after a separate investigation by ad security firm Confiant and researchers at Citizen Lab exposed how online advertising infrastructure was used to distribute spyware, highlighting another vulnerability in the digital advertising ecosystem.

That investigation, detailed in research published by Citizen Lab, found that Israeli company Discover-Tech exploited targeted advertising mechanisms to deliver zero-click spyware, including attacks against devices at Al Jazeera.

While the two operations differed in their objectives — one targeting advertising budgets, the other facilitating surveillance — both illustrate how infrastructure designed to connect advertisers with audiences can be repurposed for malicious activity.

The broader concern is that BADBOX 3.0 is another iteration of an established threat rather than an isolated incident — and that dismantling one operation does not necessarily eliminate the infrastructure or incentives behind the next.

FutureSight’s new tool can help banks of all sizes get a side-by-side idea of how their media mix stacks up against the competition.

Meta’s reported CTV expansion and AppLovin’s alleged interest in Criteo are foreboding for independent ad tech as major platforms extend their reach.

Advertisers continue to increase investments on the retail weekend but AI, tariffs and discounting pressure present banana skins.

#7272. D+ Research: Brand’s holiday marketing tactics shift in 2026

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This research is based on unique data collected from our proprietary audience of publisher, agency, brand and tech insiders. It’s available to Digiday+ members. More from the series →

This is an excerpt from our Digiday+ Research report The 2026 guide to holiday marketing strategies, including A-Frame Brands, Mastercard and Ritual, which examined which commerce channels are dominating marketers’ holiday strategies. The report is based on a survey of 90 brand and retailer professionals, as well as individual interviews with marketing executives.

Digiday’s survey found that brands and retailers plan to adjust the marketing tactics they’ll use during the 2026 holiday season in comparison to those they used in 2025.

The biggest changes are that CTV/streaming and TV ads are gaining traction in 2026, with year-over-year increases of 22 percentage points and 20 percentage points, respectively.

Forty-five percent of survey respondents said they plan to use CTV/streaming ads this holiday season, in comparison to 23% of respondents who said the same in 2025. The 2026 uptick followed a 14 percentage point dip in usage from 2024 to 2025.Meanwhile, marketers’ usage of TV ads remained relatively stable between 2024 and 2025, dropping by only 4 percentage points between 2024 and 2025, but grew by a whopping 20 percentage points in 2026. Thirty-three percent of brands and retailers said they plan to use TV ads this holiday season in comparison to 13% of marketers who said the same in 2025.

“Streaming is a really valid channel to invest in during holiday because consumption of that media spikes,” said Laura Brodie, chief growth officer at supplement company Ritual. “But participating in streaming is actually a decision for the whole year. Starting to engage just for holiday would be a risky move, especially for a smaller brand.”

“If you have the data to back it up over the course of the year showing this is a channel that’s proven and the ROI metrics associated, it can make a lot of sense to spike it during holiday,” she added.

Ari Bloom, founder and CEO of brand incubator A-Frame Brands, said that both streaming and TV ads can be effective for targeting specific demographic groups during the holidays. “Streaming and television is an effective audience conversion mechanism, especially with women over 30 years old,” Bloom said. “We’ve done a lot of work on daytime TV and on QVC. We see significantly bigger segments in the holiday time period from November through mid-December, and we always put attention on those times.”

Last year, brands’ and retailers’ most-used holiday marketing tactic was brand experiences, but that strategy fell to second place in 2026 behind streaming/CTV ads. Forty-one percent of marketers said they expect to use brand experiences as a marketing strategy during the 2026 holiday season in comparison to 50% of marketers who said the same in 2025 — a drop of 9 percentage points.

Brands and retailers are also divesting from using gift guides and influencer hauls/unboxing videos this holiday season. Marketers’ usage of gift guides decreased by 19 percentage points in 2026 vs. 2025, while their use of influencer hauls/unboxing videos fell by 14 percentage points this year in comparison to last year.

Although gift guides and brand experiences decreased in marketer usage for the 2026 holidays, they remain brands’ and retailers’ top performing holiday marketing tactics over time. Thirty-one percent of marketers said gift guides have historically performed the best for their company during the holiday season, while 28% of marketers said the same of brand experiences, putting them in the No. 1 and No. 2 ranking spots, respectively.

Although some marketers are shifting away from using gift guides for the upcoming holiday season, Ritual’s Brodie said gift guides are likely to gain importance in the AI-driven search era because AI-powered search tools often rely on best of lists and curated recommendations to answer consumer queries.

“Gift guides are very interesting in the world of AEO and thinking about LLM optimization,” Brodie said. “Overall, best of lists and gift guides are a close sister to [AI search results]. Gift guides can play an important role in holiday and especially bridging LLM research and AI overviews. So, I’m bullish about gift guides.”

Although brands and retailers are leaning into CTV/streaming ads for the 2026 holidays, those marketing tactics tied in the No. 4 spot for the best-performing holiday marketing tactic over time, while pop-up shops ranked No. 3. Influencer hauls/unboxing videos was the only marketing strategy that fell in both best historic performance and marketers’ intended use during the 2026 holiday season.

A-Frame Brands’ Bloom said brands and retailers should also consider investing in catalogs for the 2026 holiday. “Catalogs have always been very successful, and they’re heavily conversion-focused,” Bloom explained. “When they’re discount-driven and when people’s attention is on buying, you can do offers that you can only get via physical paper, and it does drive people into stores.”

As Black Friday Cyber Monday rapidly approaches, brands are most focused on acquiring new customers this holiday season.

Product discovery habits are changing, leaving marketers unsure how to pivot ahead of the biggest sales period of the year.

Walmart is allowing access to Meta’s Muse through a gateway that lets it control how AI agents browse, shop and make purchases.

#7271. Senior Analytics Engineer at Kit

We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

Our Sales team needs a dedicated data partner, and creator commerce (how creators monetize by selling digital products) is becoming a bigger part of how Kit and our creators scale. Kit is growing, so we're looking for a Senior Analytics Engineer to own Kit's sales and commerce data from end to end.

Accurate sales data gives our team a clear picture of the pipeline and the confidence to make informed decisions. That’s what makes this role so important.

Your primary focus will be Sales: the dbt models that turn our CRM data into trusted pipeline, deal, and funnel metrics, and the Omni dashboards our sales leader and Senior Revenue Operations Manager use to run the business. You'll also model creator commerce data, so we understand what creators sell and earn through Kit, and you'll pick up reporting work with our Product team, such as feature usage reporting. On a small data team, we step outside our usual lanes when the need is there, so the mix will shift over time.

You'll build for two kinds of consumers: people and AI. Teammates increasingly answer their own questions by querying our semantic layer through Omni's AI and MCP connections to AI tools. That means your models, metric definitions, and documentation need to be clear and correct enough for an AI agent to use them without a data teammate in the loop.

This is a full-time individual contributor role for someone who likes being close to the business, enjoys turning messy operational data into numbers people trust, and is happy to work on both the models and the dashboards that sit on top of them. When Sales and Product have clear, reliable data, we help more creators build more valuable businesses with Kit.

You'll report to Sam Umachi, Head of Data (Linkedin).

Sales and RevOps are your primary partners, who will rely on the models and reporting you own. You'll also partner with our Product team on reporting for features like Visual Automations and on how creators use Kit.

Within our data team, you'll work alongside a Staff Analytics Engineer who owns org-wide semantic layer governance and modeling standards, a Senior Data Engineer who owns ingestion and pipelines, and a Senior Business Analytics Partner focused on revenue opportunities. This role has no direct reports.

First Week: Work through our onboarding curriculum in Notion and meet the data team, our Senior Revenue Operations Manager, the sales team, and your product partners. Get set up in our core tools: dbt Cloud, GitHub, Amazon Redshift, Omni, HubSpot, Linear, Hightouch and Slack. Set up your AI-assisted development environment and learn the shared AI workflows our data team uses. Open and merge your first small pull request so you learn our review and deployment flow early.

First Month: Map how HubSpot objects (deals, pipelines and stages, companies, contacts, and owners) flow into our dbt models, and document the gaps. Take over day-to-day ownership of the sales models and Omni dashboards that teammates have been covering. Audit current sales reporting and list where definitions differ or numbers don't reconcile. Get familiar with how commerce data (products creators sell and the revenue they earn through Kit) lands in the warehouse today. Agree with RevOps and Sales on the most important questions they need answered. Review teammates' pull requests and contribute to our shared SQL, dbt, and documentation standards. Share a written reflection with the team on what you've learned and where you'd focus first.

First Six Months: Ship and maintain well-tested, documented dbt models for core sales metrics, such as pipeline, deal velocity, stage conversion, win rates, and the sales-led funnel. Expose those metrics through the Omni semantic layer, working within the team's governance standards. Rebuild or refresh the core sales dashboards so pipeline reviews and forecasting conversations run on numbers that don't need manual reconciliation. Resolve competing sales metric definitions by writing down the options and tradeoffs, aligning with Sales and RevOps, and documenting the outcome. Model creator-level commerce data, such as products sold, revenue earned through Kit, and conversion, in partnership with data engineering. Write semantic layer descriptions, metric definitions, and documentation that let Omni's AI and MCP-connected AI tools answer routine sales questions correctly, and test them against real questions from Sales, RevOps and Product. Get Sales to the point where routine questions are answered through self-serve, whether in Omni dashboards, Omni's AI, or AI tools connected over MCP, rather than one-off requests.

First Year: Be the recognized owner of Kit's sales data, the person Sales and RevOps go to first and trust without second-guessing. Give our revenue analysis work the sales and commerce models it depends on, so deeper questions about pipeline health and creator earnings can be answered quickly, thereby enabling us to make creators more successful. Leave your domains well documented and well tested, legible to new teammates, stakeholders, and the AI agents that query our semantic layer. Help raise the team's modeling quality through code review, pairing, and sharing what you learn.

Deep dbt experience. You build modular, tested, documented dbt projects, and you're comfortable with incremental models, snapshots, macros, CI, and scheduled jobs in dbt Cloud or similar.

Advanced SQL. You reason carefully about grain, joins, and window functions, and you write queries that are readable and performant on a cloud warehouse.

Data modeling for sales and GTM. You can model deals, pipeline stages and their history, ownership changes, and funnel conversion into clean, reusable structures. You are opinionated on Kimball standards and when a concept should be a fact or dimension.

CRM data fluency. You know how sales data behaves in a CRM, including stage changes, backdated edits, and custom properties. HubSpot is preferred, but other CRM experience (e.g. Salesforce) applies just as well. We care most that you recognize common CRM patterns.

AI-assisted development. You use AI code editors and agents (such as Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, or similar) every day, and you validate their output with tests, query results, and review before anything ships.

Clear written communication. You explain metric definitions, tradeoffs, and data caveats in plain language that Sales, RevOps, and Product partners understand.

BI and semantic layer experience. You can build dashboards and semantic models in Omni, Looker, or a similar BI tool, and you design metric definitions that people and AI tools can both query correctly.

Git-based workflows. You keep pull requests small and well described, and you give thoughtful code review.

Nice to have: Redshift performance tuning (sort and distribution keys), Python for scripting or API pulls, reverse ETL, and experience with product event, billing, payments, or ecommerce data.

Around 7 or more years in analytics engineering or data engineering where you personally shipped production data models.

Several years of hands-on work building and maintaining production dbt projects.

Working directly with sales teams and RevOps, and owning reporting they relied on to run the business.

Owning a data domain over time and evolving its models as the business and its questions changed.

Working at a SaaS company, ideally one with both a self-serve and a sales-led motion. Commerce, payments, or creator economy experience is a plus, not a requirement.

Working on a small or remote team where you were trusted to make decisions independently.

Ownership and follow-through. You treat your data domains as yours. You notice problems before stakeholders do, close the loop on requests, and finish what you start.

Judgment. You match rigor to the decision at hand, know when a quick answer is enough and when a request should become a durable model, and say so clearly.

Flexibility. You're glad to step outside your primary area when another team needs help, and you balance that work against your core commitments openly.

Teach everything you know. You document as you go, pair generously, and help stakeholders understand the data well enough to use it on their own.

Work in public. You share progress, open questions, and decisions in writing where the team can see them, including what didn't work.

Creator success is our success. You remember who's on the other end. A wrong number in a pipeline review means Sales spends time on the wrong creators, and that shapes how carefully you work.

Do it with excellence. You test your work, review it carefully, and don't ship a number you can't defend.

Candor with care. You're comfortable pushing back when a request won't answer the real question, and you do it in a way that builds trust.

Needing detailed specs or a highly structured environment before you can start.

Discomfort with ambiguity. Some of our sales and commerce definitions are still evolving, and you'll help settle them.

Needing several layers of approval to make modeling decisions within your domain.

Wanting to work only on dashboards, or only on pipelines, rather than across the transformation and BI layers.

Wanting a strictly fixed scope. Sales comes first, but you'll pick up Product or other work when the business needs it.

Wanting mostly greenfield work. Much of this role is improving sales models and dashboards that already exist.

Preferring to take tickets as written rather than partnering with stakeholders on what they need.

Being skeptical of AI's capabilities, or using AI without good judgment and accountability.

Expecting a large data team with dedicated specialists for every part of the stack.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe.
